She dropped out of bed smiling and sniffed at the roses she had worn the day before, standing in a glass on her washstand, freshened, half faded, half fresh, intoxicating as she bent over them. She dressed, without drawing back her curtains, in the soft rose-blue light, singing Mrs. Kronen’s song in an undertone.

===Chinese===
这是一封挺长的信——署名是一个大大的“鲍勃”（Bob），斜斜地划过一角。开头是请她出主意，给哈丽特（Harriett）的婚礼送什么礼好；结尾则提议由她去同他见一面，帮他做一次合适的挑选。信是从英国的国际象棋俱乐部（British Chess Club）写来的，写给她的，因为鲍勃·格雷维尔（Bob Greville）想见她。哈丽特的婚礼赠礼不过是个由头。她把信封连同两页摊开的信纸往蓝色床罩上一撂，喝完杯里的咖啡，对着杯子微笑。老鲍勃不知道，他已经给她披上了一身铠甲。他想单独见她。他们两个人要见面、要说话，不为任何缘由，只因为他们想。可是，一个觉得《考德尔太太的帐中训话》（Mrs. Caudle's Curtain Lectures）——哪怕是犊皮装帧的漂亮版本——或者《婚后如何幸福》（How to Be Happy Though Married）适合当哈丽特的婚礼赠礼、适合当任何人的婚礼赠礼的人，她还能有什么话对他说呢？不过，他们倒是可以通信。让信、让秘密的信，早晨随着早餐一起送进她这间蓝色的房间，是理所当然的事。她微笑着下了床，俯身去嗅她前一天戴过的玫瑰——它们立在盥洗台上的玻璃杯里，添过了水，半蔫半鲜，俯下去的一瞬香得醉人。她没有拉开窗帘，就在那柔和的玫瑰蓝的光里穿衣，低声哼着克罗嫩太太（Mrs. Kronen）那支歌。

---

===Original===
At eleven o’clock Mrs. Corrie swept into the schoolroom. Miriam looked easily up at her from the dreamy thicket where she and the children had spent their hour, united and content, speaking in undertones, getting easily through books that had seemed tiresome and indifferent the day before. She had felt the play of her mind on theirs and their steady adult response. They had joined as conspirators in this mad contemptible business of mastering the trick of the textbook, each dreaming the while his own dream.

“You darlings,” cried Mrs. Corrie, “how sweet you all look!” They raised drunken eyes and beamed drowsily at her. “Give them a holiday,” said Mrs. Corrie, raising her hands over the table like a conductor about to start an orchestra. “Give them a holiday﻿—a picnic﻿—and come and buy hats!”

In a moment the room was in an uproar of capering figures. “Hats! A new hat for Rollo! Heaps of cash! I’ve got heaps of cash!”

Miriam blinked from her thicket. This was anarchy; she felt herself sliding. But they were so old. All so old and experienced. She so young, by so far the youngest of the four.

===Chinese===
十一点钟，科里太太（Mrs. Corrie）一阵风似的卷进家庭教室。米丽亚姆从那片朦胧的灌木丛里从容地抬眼望她——这一个钟头，她和孩子们就待在那里面，连成一心，心满意足，压低嗓音说话，把头一天还显得又乏味又隔膜的书本轻轻松松读了过去。她觉出了自己的心思在他们心思上的流转，也觉出了他们稳稳当当、大人气的回应。他们像同谋一般，加入了掌握课本窍门这桩疯疯癫癫、上不得台面的营生，一边各做各的梦。

“你们这些小宝贝儿，”科里太太喊道，“瞧着多招人疼呀！”他们抬起醉醺醺的眼睛，睡意朦胧地朝她笑。“给他们放个假吧，”科里太太说着，双手扬到桌子上方，像一位即将起奏的乐队指挥。“放个假——去野餐——你也来，咱们买帽子去！”

转眼间，满屋子已经闹成一片蹦蹦跳跳的人影。“帽子！给罗洛（Rollo）买顶新帽子！好些个钱！我搞到好些个钱啦！”

米丽亚姆在她的灌木丛里眨了眨眼。这是无政府状态；她觉得自己正往下出溜。可他们个个都那么老。全都那么老、那么有阅历。她倒这么年轻——四个人里头，数她最小，还小出去一大截。

---

===Original===
Mrs. Corrie sat back in the victoria, her face alight under the cream lace veil she had twisted round her soft winter hat, and talked in quiet clipped phrases: soft shouts. They were driving swiftly through the fresh warmth of the April midday.

They were off for the afternoon. The commons gleamed a prelude. Miriam saw that Mrs. Corrie did not notice them nor think of sweeping back across them later on through the afternoon air and seeing them move and gleam in the afternoon light. She did not think of the bright shops, the strangely dyed artificial flowers with their curious fascinating smell interwoven with the strange warm smell of velvet and chenille and straw.﻿ ﻿… Miriam had once bought a hat in a shop in Kensington. As long as it lasted it had kept for her whenever she looked at its softly dyed curiously plaited straw something of the exciting fascination of the shop, the curious faint flat odours of millinery, the peculiar dim warm smell of silks and velvets﻿—silk, China and Japan, silkworms weaving shining threads in the dark. Even when it had become associated with outings and events and shabby with exposure it remained each time she took it afresh from its box of wrappings, a mysterious sacred thing; and the soft blending of its colours, the coiled restraint of its shape, the texture of its snuggled trimmings were a support, refreshing her thoughts. She had never known anyone who went regularly to good hatshops; the sense of them as a part of life was linked only with Mrs. Kronen﻿—Mrs. Kronen’s little close toque made of delicately shaded velvet violets and lined with satin, her silky peacock blue straw shining with rich filmy tones, its mass of dull shot blue-green ribbon and the soft rose pink of its velvet roses. These hats had excited Mrs. Corrie; the hats and the sand-coloured silk dust cloak explained her cheque and her sudden happiness. But they only made her want to buy hats. The going and the shops were nothing to her. She talked about the Kronens as they drove, speaking as though she wanted Miriam to hear without answering. “She knows Mrs. Kronen fascinates me,” thought Miriam.

“Ain’t they a pair, lordy﻿ ﻿… him divorced and her divorced and then marryin’ each other. Ain’t it scandalous, eh?”

===Chinese===
科里太太靠坐在四轮敞篷马车里，脸在奶白色蕾丝面纱下亮堂堂的——面纱是她随手缠在那顶柔软冬帽上的——嘴里说着话，一段段安安静静剪短了的短语：软软的呼喊。马车正飞快地驶过四月正午清新的暖意。

这个下午，他们出来了。荒原闪出一支序曲。米丽亚姆看出，科里太太并没有留意荒原，也没想着下午晚些时候兜回来，穿过荒原，看它们在午后的光线里浮动、闪烁。她也没有想着那些亮堂堂的铺子，那些染得古里古怪的人造花——它们那奇异的、勾人的气味，同丝绒、绳绒和草辫那陌生的暖味交织在一起。……米丽亚姆有一回在肯辛顿（Kensington）一家铺子里买过一顶帽子。帽子还在的那些日子，每当她望着它那染得柔柔的、编法古怪的草辫，那家铺子叫人心跳的魅力总还替她存着几分——女帽店里那种奇异的、淡淡的、平塌塌的气味，丝绸和丝绒那种独特的、幽幽的暖香——丝绸，中国的、日本的，蚕在黑暗里织出发亮的丝。哪怕后来它已经同出门、同种种事搭上了钩，风吹日晒地旧了，可每一回她从裹着纸的盒子里把它重新取出，它都还是一件神秘的、神圣的东西；它颜色的柔柔调和，形状的盘拢克制，饰边偎贴的质地，都是一种支撑，让她的思绪重新清爽。她从来不认识一个常去像样帽店的人；把帽店当作生活一部分的那种感觉，只同克罗嫩太太连在一起——克罗嫩太太那顶小小的、箍得紧紧的小帽，用深深浅浅的丝绒紫罗兰做成，缎子衬里；她那顶丝滑的孔雀蓝草帽，闪着浓润朦胧的光泽，一大团幽暗的蓝绿闪色缎带，还有丝绒玫瑰那柔柔的玫瑰粉。这几顶帽子让科里太太兴奋过；这几顶帽子，加上那件沙色真丝防尘斗篷，解释了她的支票和她突如其来的快乐。可它们只叫她想买帽子。去一趟、铺子本身，对她都不算什么。马车一路走，她谈着克罗嫩夫妇，那口气像是要米丽亚姆只管听、不要答。“她知道克罗嫩太太把我迷住了，”米丽亚姆想。

“瞧这俩配的一对儿，老天……他离了婚，她也离了婚，回头俩人结了婚。你说吓人不吓人，嗯？”

===Original===
People like the Corries disapproved of people like the Kronens, but had them to stay with them and were excited about their clothes. Miriam returned to listen to the singing of her body; it would sing until they got to the station. As she listened she held firmly clasped the letter she had addressed to the British Chess Club to say she would be nowhere near London until the weddings. “She doesn’t care a rap about him﻿—not a teeny rap﻿ ﻿… she’s a wise lady﻿ ﻿… dollars﻿—that’s the thing,” whispered Mrs. Corrie gaily. What does she want me to say? thought Miriam. What would she say if I pretended to agree?

Should she tell her about the weddings? Perhaps not. It would be time enough, she reflected rapidly, when she had to ask permission to go home for them. Mrs. Corrie had not asked her a single question about things at home, and if she were to say, “We used to live in a big house and my father lost nearly all his money and we live now in a tiny villa and two of my sisters are to be married,” it would break into this strange easy new life. It would break the charm and not bring her any nearer to Mrs. Corrie. And Mrs. Corrie would not really understand about the home troubles. Mrs. Corrie had always been lonely and sad, inside. She had been an orphan, but brought up by a wealthy uncle and always living in wealth and now she seemed to think about nothing but the children and the house and the garden﻿—hating theatres and dances and never going to them or paying visits or seeing the wonder of anything. She would only say, “Don’t you marry yourself off, young lady, marriage is a fraud. You wait for a wealthy one.” Whatever one said to her, whatever joy one showed her would lead to that.

But the two weddings hovered about the commons. They were a great possession. Nothing to worry about in them. Gerald and Bennett who had managed everything since the smash would manage them. Sarah and Harriet would be married from the little villa and would be Mrs. Brodie and Mrs. Ducayne just like anybody else. So safe. And she herself, free, getting interesting letters, going up to town with Mrs. Corrie, no worry, spring hats and the commons and garden waiting for them. She was sure she did not want to see the commons overburdened by the idea of her own wedding. Two was enough for the present. Of course, some day﻿—someone, somewhere, wonderful and different from everyone else. Cash﻿—no, not business and cigars and offices﻿ ﻿… the city, horrible bloated men with shapeless figures, horrible chemists’ shops advertising pick-me-ups﻿ ﻿… a cottage﻿—a cottage. Why did people laugh at love in a cottage? The outsides of cottages were the best part, everyone said. They were dark inside; but why not? A lamp; and outside the garden and the light.

===Chinese===
科里家这样的人家，看不惯克罗嫩家那样的人家，却请他们来家里做客，又为他们的衣裳兴奋。米丽亚姆回过头去听她身体的歌唱；它会一直唱到车站。她一面听，一面把那封信紧紧攥在手里——信是写给英国的国际象棋俱乐部的，说她到婚礼之前无论如何都到不了伦敦近旁。“他呀，她一星半点儿也不放在心上——针尖大点儿也不放在心上……她是个精明的太太……美元——要紧的是这个，”科里太太欢欢喜喜地悄声说。她要我说什么呢？米丽亚姆想。要是我装作同意，她又会怎么说呢？

要不要把两桩婚礼告诉她？也许不。等她到时要告假回家参加婚礼，再说不迟，她飞快地盘算着。科里太太关于她家里的情形，一个问题也没有问过；要是她说出“我们从前住一座大宅子，我父亲几乎把所有的钱都赔光了，如今我们住在一幢小小的别墅里，我的两个姐妹就要出嫁了”，就会搅破这种奇怪的、松快的新生活。那会破了魔咒，却又不能让她同科里太太更亲近一步。何况家里的难处，科里太太也未必真能懂。科里太太心里向来又寂寞又忧伤。她是个孤女，却是富有的叔父抚养大的，一直生活在富裕里，如今她仿佛什么都不想，只想着孩子、宅子和花园——讨厌戏院和舞会，从来不去，也不拜客，也看不见任何东西的了不起。她只会说：“小姑娘，你可别忙着把自己嫁掉，婚姻是个骗局。你等着挑个有钱的。”不管对她说什么，不管让她看见什么欢喜，到头来总要引到这一句上。

可两桩婚礼总在荒原上空盘旋。它们是一宗大大的家当。里头没有什么可操心的。自“破产”（the smash）以来把一切操持起来的杰拉尔德（Gerald）和贝内特（Bennett），会把它们操持妥当。萨拉（Sarah）和哈丽特会从那幢小别墅里出嫁，会成为布罗迪太太（Mrs. Brodie）和杜坎太太（Mrs. Ducayne），同随便哪一个人一样。多么安稳。而她自己呢，自由自在，收着有意思的信，随科里太太进城，无忧无虑，春天的帽子、荒原和花园都在前头等着。她确知自己不愿看见荒原让她自己婚礼的念头压得不堪重负。眼下两桩就够了。当然啦，将来有一天——某个人，在某处，妙得同所有人都不一样。钞票——不，不要生意、雪茄和写字楼……金融城，肿得走了形的可怕男人，打着提神补酒广告的可怕药房……一座小村舍——一座小村舍。人们为什么笑话茅屋里的爱情？村舍的外表是最好的部分，人人都这么说。里头是暗的；可暗又有什么不好？一盏灯；外面是花园和光。

===Original===
“She’s had all *kinds* of operations,” mused Mrs. Corrie.

“*Really?*”

“Deadly awful. In nursing homes. She’ll never have any kiddies.”

Were there cold shadows on everything, everywhere?

She turned a pleading face to Mrs. Corrie. They were driving into the station yard.

===Chinese===
“她可是什么*样*的手术都动过了，”科里太太若有所思地说。

“*真的？*”

“吓死人的可怕。在私人疗养院里。她这辈子别想有小孩儿了。”

是不是每样东西上、到处，都罩着冷冷的阴影？

她转向科里太太，一张恳求的脸。马车正驶进车站的院子。

===Original===
“It’s true, true, true,” laughed Mrs. Corrie. “She doesn’t care, she doesn’t want any. They’re all like that, that sort.”

Miriam mused intensely. She felt Mrs. Kronen ought to be there to answer. She had some secret Mrs. Corrie did not possess. Mrs. Corrie looked suddenly small and mild and funny. Why did she think it dreadful that Mrs. Kronen should have no children? There was nothing wonderful in having children. It was better to sing. She was perfectly sure that she herself did not want children.﻿ ﻿… “Superior women don’t marry,” she said, “sir she said, sir she said, su, per, *i*, or women”﻿—but that meant blue stockings.

===Chinese===
“真的呀，真的，真的，”科里太太笑道。“她不在乎，她一个也不要。全是那样的，那一类人。”

米丽亚姆使劲地想。她觉得克罗嫩太太就该在这儿，好来应上一句。她有一样科里太太没有的秘密。科里太太忽然显得又小、又温和、又滑稽。她为什么觉得克罗嫩太太没有孩子是桩可怕的事呢？生孩子有什么了不起。还是唱歌好。她完完全全拿得准，她自己就不要孩子。……“优越的女人不结婚，”她说，“先生她是这么说的，先生她是这么说的，优、越、*的*、女人”——不过那可就该是蓝袜子（女学究）了。

---

===Original===
“I don’t want a silly hat,” said Mrs. Corrie, as their hansom drew up in bright sunlight outside a milliner’s at the southern end of Regent Street. “Let’s buy a real lovely teapot or a Bartolozzi or somethin’. What fun to go home with somethin’ real nice. Eh? A real real beauty Dresden teapot,” she chanted, floating into the dimness of the shop where large hats standing on long straight stands flared softly like blossoms in the twilight.

She swept about in her flowing lace-trimmed twine-coloured overcoat on the green velvet carpet, or stood ruthlessly trying on a hat, pressing its wire frame to fit her head, crushing her fingers into tucked tulle, talking and trying, and discarding until the collection was exhausted. Miriam sat angry and admiring, wondering at the subdued helplessness of the satin-clad assistant, sorry for the discarded hats lying carelessly about, their glory dimmed. All the hats, whatever their shape or colour seemed to her to decorate the bronze head and the twine-coloured coat. The little toques gave slenderness and willowy height, and the large flowered ribboned hats, the moment a veil draped the boniness of the face made, Miriam felt, an entrancing picturesqueness. With each hat Mrs. Corrie addressed the large mirror calling herself a freak, a sketch, a nightmare, a real real fogey.

===Chinese===
“我可不想要什么傻帽子，”科里太太说，这时她们的双轮马车（hansom）在明晃晃的日头里停在了摄政街南头一家女帽店门外。“咱们买一把真正可爱的茶壶吧，要么一幅巴托洛齐（Bartolozzi）版画，要么点别的什么。带一件真正好的东西回家，多有趣。嗯？一把真真正正、漂漂亮亮的德累斯顿茶壶，”她唱着，飘进店铺的幽暗里去——一顶顶大帽子立在高高的直架上，像暮色里的花朵，柔柔地怒放。

她那件飘飘洒洒、缀着蕾丝的麻黄大衣在绿丝绒地毯上扫来扫去，要不就站定，狠着心试戴一顶帽子，把铁丝帽架往头上扣，好叫它服帖，手指头狠狠摁进打着褶的绢纱里，说着、试着、丢开着，直到那一批帽子试了个罄尽。米丽亚姆坐在一边，又气恼又叹服，纳罕那位绸缎衣裳的店员怎么那般低眉顺眼、全无办法，又替那些随随便便丢在一边、光华黯淡了的落选帽子难过。在她眼里，所有的帽子，不论什么式样、什么颜色，装点的都是那颗青铜色的头和那件麻黄大衣。小小的小帽衬出苗条，衬出杨柳般的高挑；而那些缀花结带的大帽子，只消一面面纱一拢，罩住那张脸上嶙峋的骨相，就——米丽亚姆觉得——透出一种勾人的画意。每换一顶帽子，科里太太都要对着大镜子数落一通，管自己叫怪物，叫速写，叫噩梦，叫一个不折不扣的老古板。

---

===Original===
The process seemed endless and Miriam sat at last scourging herself with angry questions. “Why doesn’t she decide,” she found herself repeating almost aloud, her hot tired eyes turning for relief to the soft guipure-edged tussore curtain screening the lower part of the window, “what kind of hat she really wants and then look at the few most like it and perhaps have one altered?﻿ ﻿…” “It’s so awfully silly not to have a plan. She’ll go on simply forever.” But the soft curtain running so evenly along its smooth clean brass rod was restful, and plan or no plan the trouble would presently come to an end and there would be no discomforts to face when it was over﻿—no vulgar bun shops, no struggling on to a penny bus with your ride perhaps spoiled by a dreadful neighbour, but Regent Street in the bright sun, a hansom, a smart obliging driver with a buttonhole, skimming along to tea somewhere, the first-class journey home, the carriage at the station, the green commons.

“Perhaps,” said the assistant at last in a cheerful suggestive furious voice, flinging aside with just Mrs. Corrie’s cheerful abandon, a large cream lace hat with a soft fresh mass of tiny banksia roses under its left brim, “Perhaps moddom will allow me to make her a shape and trim it to her own design.”

Mrs. Corrie stood arrested in the middle of the green velvet floor. Wearily Miriam faced the possibility of the development of this fresh opportunity for going on forever.

===Chinese===
这个过程仿佛没有尽头，米丽亚姆末了坐在那儿，拿气冲冲的质问抽打自己。“她怎么就不定下来，”她发觉自己几乎出了声地反复念叨，一双滚烫的倦眼转开去，在挡着窗户下半截的那幅镶凸纹花边（guipure）的柞丝软帘上寻一点松快，“她到底真心想要什么样式的帽子，然后拣最相近的几顶看看，兴许再照着改一顶？……”“连个章程都没有，蠢得没边儿了。她会这么没完没了地试下去。”可那幅软帘沿着光洁的黄铜杆走得那么匀停，看着就叫人心安；再说，有章程没章程，这场别扭转眼总要到头，到头以后就再没有什么难堪要对付了——不必进俗气的面包圈铺子，不必挤上一便士的公共马车、让一位可怕的邻座客搅了这一程，有的是亮堂堂太阳底下的摄政街，一辆双轮马车，一位扣眼簪花、又时新又殷勤的车夫，一路轻快地滑去什么地方吃茶点，头等车厢回家，车站上迎候的马车，绿色的荒原。

“要不这样，”店员终于开口了，嗓音又欢快、又献策、又狂怒，把一顶大大的奶色蕾丝帽子往旁边一甩——左帽檐底下软软嫩嫩一大簇小小的木香花（banksia roses）——那股兴冲冲甩开的劲头，同科里太太一模一样，“要不这样，太太能不能容我给您做一顶帽坯，照您自己的设计来镶？”

科里太太在绿丝绒地毯正中央钉住了似的站定。米丽亚姆疲惫地直面起这个前景来：这崭新的机会再发展下去，又是永无止境。

===Original===
“Wouldn’t that be lovely?” said Mrs. Corrie, turning to her enthusiastically.

“Yes,” said Miriam eagerly. Both women were facing her and she felt that anything would be better than their united contemplation of her brown stuff dress with its square sleeves and her brown straw hat with black ribbon and its yellow paper buttercups.

“Can’t be did though,” said Mrs. Corrie in a cold level voice, turning swiftly back to the hats massed in a confused heap on the mahogany slab. Standing over them and tweaking at one and another as she spoke she made a quiet little speech, indicating that such and such might do for the garden and such others for driving, some dozen altogether she finally ordered to be sent at once to an address in Brook Street where she would make her final selection whilst the messenger waited. “Have you got the address all right?” she wound up; “*so* kind of you.” “Come along, you poor thing you look worn out,” she cried to Miriam, without looking at her as she swept from the shop. She waved her sunshade at a passing hansom and as it drew sharply up with an exciting clatter near the curb she grasped Miriam’s arm, “Shall we try Perrin’s? It’s only three doors up.” Miriam glanced along and caught a glimpse of another hat shop. “Do you really want to?” she suggested reluctantly. “No! No! not a bit old spoil sport. Chum yong, jump in,” laughed Mrs. Corrie.

“Oh, if you really want to,” began Miriam, but Mrs. Corrie, singing out the address to the driver was putting her into the cab and showing her how to make an easy passage for the one who gets last into a hansom by slipping into the near corner. Her appreciation of this little manoeuvre helped her over her contrition and she responded with gay insincerity to Mrs. Corrie’s assurance of the fun they would have over the hats at Mrs. Kronen’s.﻿ ﻿… Tea at Mrs. Kronen’s then. How strange and alarming﻿ ﻿… but she felt too tired to sustain a tête-à-tête at a smart tea shop. “After tea we’ll drop into a china shop and get somethin’ real nice,” said Mrs. Corrie excitedly, as they bowled up Regent Street.

===Chinese===
“那岂不太可爱了？”科里太太说，热切地转过身来对着她。

“好的。”米丽亚姆连忙说。两个女人都面朝着她，她只觉得随便什么都好过她们俩一齐端详她这条方袖的棕色呢裙，和她那顶黑缎带、缀黄纸毛茛的棕色草帽。

“可那哪儿成呢，”科里太太说，声音又冷又平，倏地转回身，去对付红木案板上乱作一堆的帽子。她站在那一堆上方，一边说一边这顶揪一下、那顶扯一下，发表了一小篇安静的讲话，意思是这样的几顶在园子里戴戴也许行，那样的几顶坐马车戴合适，前前后后拢共一打上下，末了吩咐立刻送到布鲁克街（Brook Street）的一个地址，她要在那儿当着信差的面做最后的定夺。“地址都记清楚了？”她收了尾，“多谢*您*啦。”“走吧，你这可怜虫，瞧你都累垮了，”她朝米丽亚姆喊，人已经一阵风扫出了店门，看也不看她一眼。她朝一辆驶过的双轮马车挥阳伞，车带着一阵撩人的咔嗒声利利索索地收在路缘近旁，她一把攥住米丽亚姆的胳膊，“咱们上佩兰店（Perrin's）去碰碰？往上才三个门脸儿。”米丽亚姆顺着街面望过去，瞥见又一家帽店。“你当真想去？”她迟迟疑疑地建议。“不！不！半点儿也不扫你的兴，老搅家精才不呢。Chum yong（来呀来呀），跳上车，”科里太太笑道。

“哦，你要是真想去，”米丽亚姆刚开头，科里太太已经朝车夫唱出了地址，一面把她往车厢里塞，一面指点她，后上双轮马车的人要进得从容，有个法子——先溜进靠里侧的角落就是。她看出了这套小手法的妙处，这一看把她的歉疚也给看散了；科里太太担保说，到了克罗嫩太太那儿，对着这些帽子会有多好玩，她也欢欢喜喜、言不由衷地应承着。……这么说，要在克罗嫩太太家吃茶点了。多么奇怪、多么叫人心慌……可她累得撑不住了，再撑不起时髦茶室里的一场对坐。“吃过茶点，咱们顺脚溜进一家瓷器店，买点真正好的东西，”科里太太兴冲冲地说，马车正一路轻快地驶上摄政街。

---

===Original===
They found Mrs. Kronen in a mauve and white drawing-room, reclining on a mauve and white striped settee in a pale mauve tea gown. On a large low table a frail mauve tea service stood ready, and Mrs. Kronen rose tall to welcome them dropping on to the mauve carpet a little volume bound in pale green velvet. On a second low table were strawberries in a shallow wide bowl, a squat jug brimming with cream, dark wedding cake hiding a pewter plate, a silken bag unloosed, showing marvellous large various sweetmeats heavy against its silk lining. As Mrs. Kronen slurred her fingers across Miriam’s hand she ordered the manservant who had dipped and gathered up the green velvet volume to ask for the teacakes.

===Chinese===
她们在一间紫红夹白的客厅里寻见了克罗嫩太太——她斜倚在一张紫红白条纹的长靠椅上，穿一件淡紫红的茶会袍。一张低低的大桌上，一套薄薄的紫红茶具已经摆开，克罗嫩太太高高地起身相迎，一本小小的书从她手里跌落在紫红地毯上——淡绿丝绒装帧。第二张矮桌上，浅浅的宽口碗里盛着草莓，一把矮墩墩的奶壶满得冒尖，深色的婚礼蛋糕底下盖着一只白镴盘，一只丝袋敞开了口，露出各色各样、又大又妙的蜜饯，沉甸甸坠着丝衬里。克罗嫩太太的手指含含混混地从米丽亚姆手上拂过，一面吩咐那已经俯身拾起绿丝绒小书的男仆，去要茶点饼。

---

===Original===
Then this was “Society.” To come so easily up from the Corries’ beautiful home, via the West End hat shop to this wonderful West End flat and eat strawberries in April.﻿ ﻿… If only the home people could see. Her fatigue vanished. Secure from Mrs. Kronen’s notice she sat in a mauve and white striped chair and contemplated her surroundings.

While they were waiting for the teacakes, Mrs. Kronen trailed about the mauve floor reciting her impressions of the weather. “So lovely,” she intoned in her curious half-Cockney. “I almost﻿—went﻿—out. But I haven’t. I﻿—haven’t﻿—stirred. It is lovely inside on this sort of spring day﻿—the *light*.”

She paused and swept about. There *is* something about her, thought Miriam. It’s true, the light inside on a clear spring day.﻿ ﻿… I never thought of that. It is somehow spring in here in the middle of London in some real way. Her blood leaped and sang as it had done driving across the commons; but even more sweetly and keenly. It wouldn’t be, in a dingy room, even in the country.﻿ ﻿… It’s an essence﻿—something you feel in the right surroundings.﻿ ﻿… What chances these people have. They get the most out of everything. Get everything in advance and over and over again. They can go into the country any minute as well as have clear light rooms. Nothing is ever grubby. And London there, all round; London﻿ ﻿… London was a soft, sea-like sound; a sound shutting in the spring. The spring gleamed and thrilled through everything in the pure bright room.﻿ ﻿… She hoped Mrs. Kronen would say no more about the light. Light, light, light. As the manservant brewed the tea and the silver teapot shone in the light as he moved it﻿—silver and strange black splashes of light﻿—caught and moving in the room. Drawing off her gloves she felt as if she could touch the flowing light.﻿ ﻿… Flowing in out of the dawn, moving and flowing and brooding and changing all day, in rooms. Mrs. Kronen was back on her settee sitting upright in her mauve gown, all strong soft curves. “That play of *Wilde’s*﻿ ﻿…” she said. Miriam shook at the name. “You ought not to miss it. He﻿—has﻿—such﻿—*genius*.” *Wilde*﻿ ﻿… *Wilde*﻿ ﻿… a play in the spring﻿—someone named Wilde. Wild spring. That was genius. There was something in the name.﻿ ﻿… “Never go to the theatre; never, never, never,” Mrs. Corrie was saying, “too much of a bore.” Genius﻿ ﻿… genius is an infinite capacity for taking pains. Capacity. A silly definition; like a proverb﻿—made up by somebody who wanted to explain.﻿ ﻿… Wylde, Wilde.﻿ ﻿… Spring.﻿ ﻿… Genius.

===Chinese===
原来这就是“上流社会”。从科里家美丽的宅子，取道西区的帽店，就这么轻而易举地上到这间奇妙的西区公寓，在四月里吃草莓。……家里人要是能看见就好了。她的疲惫烟消云散。她坐在一把紫红白条纹的椅子里，躲开了克罗嫩太太的注意，只管打量她的四下。

等茶点饼的工夫，克罗嫩太太曳着裙裾在紫红的地毯上来回逡巡，朗诵着她对天气的种种观感。“真可爱，”她用她那古怪的半伦敦腔拖腔念道。“我差一点儿——出——去了。可我没去。我——还——没——动窝呢。这样的春日，待在屋里真美——这*光线*。”

她顿住，又曳着身子兜了个转。她身上确实有点什么，米丽亚姆想。是真的，晴朗的春日，屋里的光。……这个我从来没想过。在伦敦的正当中，这间屋里不知怎么真真地就是春天。她的血跳跃起来、歌唱起来，同乘车穿过荒原时一个样；只是更甜、更锐利。在昏暗的屋里就不会这样，哪怕在乡间。……这是一种精华——要身在合适的环境里才感觉得到的东西。……这些人占着多少机会啊。他们把一切里的好处都榨到了头。样样东西都预先到手，而且到了一遍又一遍。他们随时可以进乡间去，也同样有亮堂清爽的房间。从没有一样东西是脏兮兮的。而伦敦就在那儿，四面八方都是；伦敦……伦敦是一种软软的、海一样的声音；一个把春天围拢在内的声音。春天在这间纯净明亮的屋子里穿过一切，闪着、颤着。……她希望克罗嫩太太别再谈光了。光，光，光。男仆沏着茶，银茶壶随他一动就在光里发亮——银亮，还有一片片奇怪的黑光斑——捉在房间里，游动着。她褪下手套，觉得自己仿佛摸得着那流动的光。……从黎明流进来，一整天在各个房间里移动着、流淌着、低回着、变幻着。克罗嫩太太已经回到长靠椅上，紫红袍子里坐得笔直，通身结实的软曲线。“王尔德（Wilde）的那出戏……”她说。米丽亚姆听到这名字一哆嗦。“你可不该错过。他——有——那样的——*天才*。”Wilde……Wilde……春天里的一出戏——一个姓王尔德的人。Wild spring（狂野的春天）。这就是天才。这名字里有点什么。……“从不进戏院；从不，从不，从不，”科里太太正说着呢，“腻烦得受不了。”天才……天才就是下无穷苦功的能力。能力。一个蠢定义；跟谚语似的——哪个一心要解释什么的人编出来的。……Wylde，Wilde。……春天。……天才。

---

===Original===
The little feast was over and Mrs. Kronen was puffing at a cigarette when the hats were announced. As the fine incense reached her Miriam regretted that she had not confessed to being a smoker. The suggestion of tobacco brought the charm of the afternoon to its height. When the magic of the scented cloud drew her eyes to Mrs. Kronen’s face it was almost intolerable in its keenness. She gazed wondering whether Mrs. Kronen felt so nearly wild with happiness as she did herself.﻿ ﻿… Life what are you﻿—what is life? she almost said aloud. The face was uplifted as it had been in the photograph, but with all the colour, the firm bows of gold hair, the colour in the face and strong white pillar of neck, the eyes closed instead of staring upwards and the rather full mouth flattened and drooping with its weight into a sort of tragic shapeliness﻿—like some martyr﻿ ﻿… that picture by Rossetti, *Beata Beatrix*, thought Miriam﻿ ﻿… perfect reality. She liked Mrs. Kronen for smoking like that. She was not doing it for show. She would have smoked in the same way if she had been alone. She probably wished she was, as Mrs. Corrie did not smoke. How she must have hated missing her smokes at Newlands, unless she had smoked in her room.

“It’s﻿—a﻿—mis‑take,” said Mrs. Kronen incredulously, in response to the man’s announcement of the arrival of the hats. She waved her cigarette “imperiously,” thought Miriam, “how she enjoys showing off”﻿ ﻿… to and fro in time with her words. Mrs. Corrie rose laughing and explaining and apologising. Waving her cigarette about once more Mrs. Kronen ordered the hats to be brought in and her maid to be summoned, but retained her expression of vexed incredulity. She’s simply longing for us to be off now, thought Miriam, and changed her opinion a few moments later when Mrs. Kronen, assuming on the settee the reclining position in which they had found her when they came in, disposed one by one of the hats as Mrs. Corrie and the maid freed them from their boxes and wrappings, with a little flourish of the cigarette and a few slow words.﻿ ﻿… “Im‑poss‑i‑ble; not‑in‑key‑with‑your‑lines; slightly *too ingénue*,” etc.: to three or four she gave a grudging approval, whereupon Mrs. Corrie who was laughing and pouncing from box to box would stand upright and pace holding the favoured hat rakishly on her head. The selection was soon made and Miriam, whose weariness had returned with the millinery, was sent off to instruct the messenger that three hats had been selected and a bill might be sent to Brook Street in the morning.

===Chinese===
小小的盛宴收了场，仆人来报帽子送到时，克罗嫩太太正就着一支烟喷云吐雾。那缕细细的馨香飘到她面前，米丽亚姆后悔不曾招认自己也是个抽烟的。烟草的这一提点，把这一下午的魅力推上了顶。那片香云的魔力把她的眼睛引到克罗嫩太太脸上时，那锋锐几乎叫人受不住。她凝望着，心里纳罕，克罗嫩太太是不是也跟她自己一样，快活得几乎要发狂。……生活啊，你是什么——什么是生活？她差点说出声来。那张脸仰着，同相片里一样，只是颜色俱全——金发结成的一圈圈实弓弓的弧，脸上的血色，脖颈那根结实的白柱子——眼睛闭着，不再向上凝望，稍厚的嘴让自身的重量压得平平地垂下来，垂成一种悲剧的俏丽——像哪一位殉道者……罗塞蒂那幅画，《至福的贝雅特丽齐》（*Beata Beatrix*），米丽亚姆想……完美的真实。她喜欢克罗嫩太太这么抽烟。她不是抽给人看的。哪怕独自一人，她也会这么抽。她说不定正盼着独自一人呢，因为科里太太不抽烟。在纽兰兹错过了多少回烟，她一定恨透了，除非她都是在自己房里抽的。

“这——是——弄——错了，”克罗嫩太太满心难以置信地说，算是回应男仆关于帽子到的通报。她挥着烟卷，“威风八面地，”米丽亚姆想，“她多爱显派啊”……一下一下，合着她话语的节拍。科里太太站起身，笑着、解说着、赔着不是。克罗嫩太太又把烟卷挥了一挥，吩咐把帽子搬进来，把她的女仆叫来，脸上却还端着那副又恼又难以置信的神气。她这会儿就是巴不得咱们快走，米丽亚姆想；没过几分钟她又改了主意——克罗嫩太太在长靠椅上摆回了她们进门时撞见她的那个斜倚的姿势，科里太太和女仆把帽子从纸盒和包裹里一顶顶解放出来，她便夹着烟卷小小一挥、缓缓几个字，一顶一顶地打发。……“不——可——能；同——你的——线条——不——成调；稍稍*太青涩*了点儿（ingénue）”，云云：有三四顶她勉强点了头，于是正笑着从盒扑到盒的科里太太便直起身，把那顶得宠的帽子歪戴在头上，踱起步来。挑选三两下就定了，米丽亚姆——她的疲惫已随着这一场帽子戏一并回来——被打发去交代信差：选定了三顶，账单明早可以送到布鲁克街。

===Original===
As she was treating with the messenger in the little mauve and white hall, Mrs. Corrie came out and tapped her on the shoulder. Turning, Miriam found her smiling and mysterious. “We’re going by the 5:30,” she whispered. “Would you like to go for a walk for half an hour and come back *here*?”

“*Rather!*” said Miriam heartily, with a break in her voice and feeling utterly crushed. The beautiful clear room. She loved it and belonged to it. She was turned out. “All right,” smiled Mrs. Corrie encouragingly and disappeared. Under the eyes of the messenger and the servants who were coming out of the boudoir laden with hat boxes, she got herself out through the door.

===Chinese===
她正在那间紫红夹白的小门厅里同信差交涉，科里太太走出来，在她肩上轻轻一拍。米丽亚姆回过身，见她笑盈盈的，神神秘秘。“咱们坐五点半的车走，”她悄声说。“你想不想出去散上半点钟的步，再回*这儿*来？”

“*那可太愿意了！*”米丽亚姆由衷地说，嗓音里却带着一道裂口，只觉得自己整个人被碾垮了。这间美丽的、明净的房间。她爱它，她属于它。她却被请了出去。“好嘞，”科里太太鼓励地笑着，人没了影。在信差和正抱着帽盒从闺房里出来的仆人们的眼皮底下，她把自己弄出了那扇门。

## VI / VI

===Original===
The West End street﻿ ﻿… grey buildings rising on either side, feeling away into the approaching distance﻿—angles sharp against the sky﻿ ﻿… softened angles of buildings against other buildings﻿ ﻿… high moulded angles soft as crumb, with deep undershadows﻿ ﻿… creepers fraying from balconies﻿ ﻿… strips of window blossoms across the buildings, scarlet, yellow, high up; a confusion of lavender and white pouching out along a dipping sill﻿ ﻿… a wash of green creeper up a white painted house front﻿ ﻿… patches of shadow and bright light.﻿ ﻿… Sounds of visible near things streaked and scored with broken light as they moved, led off into untraced distant sounds﻿ ﻿… chiming together.

===Chinese===
西区的街……灰色的高楼在两侧拔起，摸索着退远，退进迎面而来的远方——棱角锐利地衬着天空……楼与楼相叠处柔下来的棱角……高高的塑出线脚的檐角，酥软得像面包屑，底下藏着重重的暗影……爬藤从阳台上披散下来……一带一带的窗花横过高楼，猩红的，明黄的，高高在上；一溜淡紫和雪白，鼓鼓囊囊挤在一道下斜的窗台上……一面刷白的前墙上漫过一片绿藤……一块一块的阴影和亮光。……近处那些看得见的东西的声响，随着它们移动，被碎光描了一道道、刻了一痕痕，引出去，接上无从追踪的远方的声响……叮叮地合在一起。

---

===Original===
Wide golden streaming Regent Street was quite near. Some near narrow street would lead into it.

===Chinese===
宽阔的、金色的、奔流的摄政街就在近旁。随便哪条近旁的窄街都会通进它去。

---

===Original===
Flags of pavement flowing along﻿—smooth clean grey squares and oblongs, faintly polished, shaping and drawing away﻿—sliding into each other.﻿ ﻿… I am part of the dense smooth clean paving stone﻿ ﻿… sunlit; gleaming under dark winter rain; shining under warm sunlit rain, sending up a fresh stony smell﻿ ﻿… always there﻿ ﻿… dark and light﻿ ﻿… dawn, stealing.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
人行道的一面面石板流淌过去——平滑干净的灰色方块和长方，微微磨出光，成着形、引着路退开去——你滑进我、我滑进你。……我是这密实、平滑、干净的路石的一部分……阳光晒着；深色的冬雨下泛着光；暖和的太阳雨里亮闪闪，蒸起一股新鲜的石头的气味……永远在那里……暗了又亮……黎明，悄悄地来。……

---

===Original===
Life streamed up from the close dense stone. With every footstep she felt she could fly.

===Chinese===
生命从密实的石头里涌上来。每踩下一步，她都觉得自己能飞。

---

===Original===
The little dignified high-built cut-through street, with its sudden walled-in church, swept round and opened into brightness and a clamour of central sounds ringing harshly up into the sky.

===Chinese===
那条小小的、气派的、高楼夹峙的近道，带着它那座突然围出墙来的教堂，一转弯，豁然开朗，涌进一片亮光和一阵当中心的喧声，刺耳地震上天去。

---

===Original===
The pavement of heaven.

To walk along the radiant pavement of sunlit Regent Street forever.

===Chinese===
天堂的人行道。

在阳光照耀的摄政街那灿烂的人行道上，永远走下去。

---

===Original===
She sped along looking at nothing. Shops passed by, bright endless caverns screened with glass﻿ ﻿… the bright teeth of a grand piano running along the edge of its darkness, a cataract of light pouring down its raised lid; forests of hats; dresses, shining against darkness, bright headless crumpling stalks; sly, silky, ominous furs; metals, cold and clanging, brandishing the light; close prickling fire of jewels﻿ ﻿… strange people who bought these things, touched and bought them.

===Chinese===
她一路疾走，什么也不看。铺子一间间过去，明亮的、无尽的洞穴，隔着玻璃挡着……一架大钢琴亮亮的琴齿沿着它黑沉沉的边缘跑，掀起的地板上倾下一道光的瀑布；帽子的森林；裙衫，衬着黑暗发亮，一茎茎亮亮的、没有头的、簌簌皱缩的花茎；狡猾的、丝滑的、不祥的皮草；金属，又冷又铿锵，挥舞着光；珠宝密密匝匝、刺刺扎扎的火……买这些东西的奇怪人们，摸一摸，就买了。

---

===Original===
She pulled up sharply in front of a window. The pavement round it was clear, allowing her to stand rooted where she had been walking, in the middle of the pavement, in the midst of the tide flowing from the clear window, a soft fresh tide of sunlit colours﻿ ﻿… clear green glass shelves laden with shapes of fluted glass, glinting transparencies of mauve and amber and green, rose-pearl and milky blue, welded to a flowing tide, freshening and flowing through her blood, a sea rising and falling with her breathing.

===Chinese===
她在一扇橱窗前猛地收住脚。橱窗四周的人行道空空的，由得她把脚扎在她正走着的地方，站在人行道正中，站在从那扇明净的橱窗里流出来的潮水当中，一道柔和新鲜的、阳光颜色的潮水……明净的绿玻璃架子，满载着一件件车出凹槽的玻璃，紫红的、琥珀的、绿的、玫瑰珠光的和乳蓝的，一泓泓莹莹的透明，焊进一道流动的潮水里，一阵阵新起来，流过她的血，一片海，随她的呼吸一起一落。

---

===Original===
The edge had gone from the keenness of the light. The street was a happy, sunny, simple street﻿—small. She was vast. She could gather up the buildings in her arms and push them away, clearing the sky﻿ ﻿… a strange darkling and she would sleep. She felt drowsy, a drowsiness in her brain and limbs and great strength, and hunger.

A clock told her she had been away from Brook Street ten minutes. Twenty minutes to spare. What should she do with her strength? Talk to someone or write﻿ ﻿… Bob; where was Bob? Somewhere in the West End. She would write from the West End a note to him in the West End.

===Chinese===
光的锋刃已经从那锐利里退了出去。这条街成了一条快活的、阳光的、简简单单的街——小小的。她浩大。她能把两边的楼群拢进臂弯，一把推开，清出整片天空……一阵奇怪的暗下来，她就要睡了。她觉得困，一阵困意漫上头脑和四肢，还有大力气，还有饥饿。

一座钟告诉她，她离开布鲁克街（Brook Street）已经十分钟了。还富余二十分钟。她该拿她的力气做什么用呢？同谁说说话，要不写点什么……鲍勃；鲍勃在哪儿呢？西区里的什么地方。她要从西区，给人在西区的他，写一张便条。

---

===Original===
There were no cheap shops in Regent Street. She looked about. Across the way a little side street showing a small newspaper shop offered help.

===Chinese===
摄政街上没有便宜的铺子。她四下张望。街对面一条小横巷里露着一爿小报店，递上了援手。

---

===Original===
Thoroughly frightened she hurried with clenched hands down the little mean street ready to give up her scheme at the first sight of an unfriendly eye. “We went through those *awful* side streets off the West End; I was *terrified*; I didn’t know *where* he was driving us,” Mrs. Poole had said about a cabman driving to the theatre﻿ ﻿… and her face as she sat in her thick pink dress by the dining-room fire had been cunning and mean and full of terror. A small shop appeared close at hand, there were newspaper posters propped outside it and its window was full of flyblown pipes, toilet requisites, stationery and odd-looking books. “Letters may be left here,” said a dirty square of cardboard in the corner of the window. “That’s all right,” thought Miriam, “it’s a sort of agency.” She plunged into the gloomy interior. “*Yes!*” shouted a tall stout man with a red coarse face coming forward, as if she had asked something that had made him angry. “I want some notepaper, just a little, the smallest quantity you have and an envelope,” said Miriam, quivering and panic-stricken in the hostile atmosphere. The man turned and whisked a small packet off a shelf, throwing it down on the counter before her. “One penny!” bellowed the man as she took it up. “Oh, thank you,” murmured Miriam ingratiatingly putting down twopence. “Do you sell pencils?” The man’s great fingers seemed an endless time wrenching a small metal-sheathed pencil from its card. The street outside would have closed in and swallowed her up forever if she did not quickly get away.

===Chinese===
她吓得透透的，攥紧两手，急匆匆顺着那条寒碜的小街走，一眼望见不友善的眼色就准备放弃她的计划。“我们穿过西区外面那些*可怕*的横街；我*吓坏*了；我*根本不知道*他要把我们拉到哪儿去，”普尔太太（Mrs. Poole）说过这么一句，说的是马车夫拉她们去戏院……她穿着厚厚的粉红裙坐在饭厅炉火边的那张脸，又精、又刻薄、又盛满了恐惧。一爿小店在近旁出现了，门外支着报纸招贴，橱窗里摆满落了蝇屎的烟斗、盥洗用品、文具和样子古怪的书。“信件可留存此处，”橱窗角落里一块脏兮兮的方纸板说。“这就妥了，”米丽亚姆想，“算是半个代办所。”她一头扎进幽暗的店堂。“*要什么！*”一个高大粗壮、红脸膛粗皮肤的男人迎上来吼道，仿佛她问了什么惹恼了他的话。“我要点信纸，就要一点，要你们最小的一份，再加一个信封，”米丽亚姆说，在敌意的空气里发着抖、慌了神。男人转过身，从架子上唰地抽下一小包，撂在她面前的柜台上。“一个便士！”她刚拿起来，男人就吼。“哦，谢谢您，”米丽亚姆低声下气地咕哝，放下两便士。“你们卖铅笔吗？”男人巨大的手指似乎花了无穷的时间，才把一支小小的金属壳铅笔从它的纸卡上拧下来。外头的街道眼看就要合拢过来，把她一口吞下、永不吐还，要是她不赶紧走脱的话。

---

===Original===
“Dear Mr. Greville,” she wrote in a clear bold hand.﻿ ﻿… He won’t expect me to have that kind of handwriting, like his own, but stronger. He’ll admire it on the page and then hear a man’s voice, Pater’s voice talking behind it and not like it. Me. He’d be a little afraid of it. She felt her hard self standing there as she wrote, and shifted her feet a little, raising one heel from the ground, trying to feminise her attitude; but her hat was hard against her forehead, her clothes would not flow.﻿ ﻿… “Just imagine that I am in town﻿—I could have helped you with your shopping if I had known I was coming.﻿ ﻿…” The first page was half filled. She glanced at her neighbours, a woman on one side and a man on the other, both bending over telegram forms in a careless preoccupied way﻿—wealthy, with expensive clothes with West End lines.﻿ ﻿… Regent Street was Salviati’s. It was Liberty’s and a music shop and the shop with the chickens. But most of all it was Salviati’s. She feared the officials behind the long grating could see by the expression of her shoulders that she was a scrubby person who was breaking the rules by using one of the little compartments with its generosity of ink and pen and blotting paper, for letter writing. Someone was standing impatiently just behind her, waiting for her place. “*Telle est la vie*,” she concluded with a flourish, “yours sincerely,” and addressed the envelope in almost illegible scrawls. Guiltily she bought a stamp and dropped the letter with a darkening sense of guilt into the box. It fell with a little muffled plop that resounded through her as she hurried away towards Brook Street. She walked quickly, to make everything surrounding her move more quickly. London revelled and clamoured softly all round her; she strode her swiftest heightening its clamorous joy. The West End people, their clothes, their carriages and hansoms, their clean bright spring-filled houses, their restaurants and the theatres waiting for them this evening, their easy way with each other, the mysterious something behind their faces, was hers. She, too, now had a mysterious secret face﻿—a West End life of her own.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
“亲爱的格雷维尔先生，”她用一手清朗遒劲的字写道。……他不会想到我有这样的字，同他的字像，却更硬。他会在这页纸上赏识它，然后听见一个男人的声音，爸爸（Pater）的声音在它背后说话，并且不喜欢它。我。他会有一点点怕它。她一面写，一面觉出她那个硬邦邦的自我站在那儿，便把脚挪了挪，一只脚跟从地上抬起一点儿，想叫自己的姿势女人气一些；可帽子硬硬地抵着她的额头，衣裳不肯流动。……“只当是我在城里——早知道我要来，就能陪您置办东西了。……”第一页写了一半。她瞥了瞥两旁的人，一边一个女人，一边一个男人，都漫不经心、心事重重地俯在电报纸上——有钱人，一身带着西区剪裁的贵重衣裳。……摄政街就是萨尔维亚蒂玻璃店（Salviati's）。它是利伯提商店（Liberty's），是一家音乐商店，是那家卖鸡的铺子。可最最要紧的，它是萨尔维亚蒂玻璃店。她怕长格栅后面的职员们能从她肩膀的表情里看出来，她是个寒酸的违规者，占着一个小隔间，拿它那大方的墨水、钢笔和吸墨纸来写信。有人正不耐烦地立在她背后，等她的位子。“人生如是（*Telle est la vie*），”她甩了一个花哨的收笔，“您真诚的，”然后用几乎认不出来的潦草字写好信封。她心怀愧疚地买了一枚邮票，怀着越压越沉的负罪感把信投进邮筒。信落下去，噗的一声轻闷响，穿过她的全身回荡着，她已匆匆朝布鲁克街走去。她走得飞快，好叫她四周的一切也动得快些。伦敦在她四下软软地欢腾、喧闹；她迈开最快的步子，把那喧闹的欢喜又扬高一层。西区的人们，他们的衣裳，他们的马车和双轮马车，他们明亮干净、盛满春天的宅子，今晚等着他们的餐馆和戏院，他们彼此之间那副从容，他们脸后面那种神秘的东西，都是她的了。她呢，如今也有了一张神秘的、藏着秘密的脸——一段她自己的西区生活。……

## VII / VII

===Original===
The next morning there was a letter from Bob containing a page of description of his dull afternoon at his club within half a mile of her. “Let me know, my dear girl,” it went on, “whenever you escape from your gaolers, and do not suffer the thought of old Bob’s making himself responsible for all the telegrams you may send to cloud your joyous young independence.”

Miriam recoiled from the thought of a dull bored man looking to her for enlivenment of the moving coloured wonder of London and felt that Mr. and Mrs. Corrie were anything but gaolers. She was not sorry that she had missed the opportunity of seeing him. “Meanwhile write and tell me your thoughts,” was the only sentence that had appealed to her in the letter; but she was sure she could not wholeheartedly offer her thoughts as entertainment to a man who spent his time feeling dull in a club. He’s﻿ ﻿… blasé, that’s it, she reflected. Perhaps it would be better not to write again. He’s not my sort a bit, she pondered with a sudden dim sense of his view of her as a dear girl. But she knew she wanted to retain him to decorate her breakfast tray with letters.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，有一封鲍勃的信，写满一页，描述他在俱乐部里度过的那个乏味的下午——俱乐部就在离她半英里之内。“只消你从狱卒们手里逃脱出来，就告诉我，亲爱的姑娘，”信接着写道，“也别让一个念头来搅扰你：说老鲍勃要挺身担干系，担下你兴许会拍来的所有的电报，搅浑你那快活的、年轻的独立自主。”

想到一个乏味腻烦的男人指望她来给流动的、彩色的伦敦奇观添一点生趣，米丽亚姆往后一缩，并且觉得科里先生和科里太太怎么也算不上狱卒。错过了见他的机会，她并不遗憾。“这其间，写信把你的想法告诉我，”是整封信里唯一入了她耳的一句；可她拿得准，她没法一心一意地拿自己的想法去取悦一个把光阴消磨在俱乐部里发腻的男人。他是……什么都腻了（blasé），就是这个，她思量道。也许还是不要再写的好。他压根儿不是我这一路人，她琢磨着，心头忽然淡淡掠过他在她身上看见的那个“亲爱的姑娘”。可她知道自己想把他留用着，好让一封封来信装点她的早餐托盘。

---

===Original===
The following day Mrs. Corrie decided that she did not want to keep the hats. She would spend the money intended for them on sketching lessons. An artist should come once a week and teach them all to paint from Nature. This decision excited Miriam deeply, putting everything else out of her mind. It promised the satisfaction of a desire she had cherished with bitter hopelessness ever since her schooldays when every Friday had brought the necessity of choking down her longing to join the little crowd of girls who took “extras” and filed carelessly in to spend a magic afternoon amongst easels and casts in the large room. The old longing came leaping back higher than it had ever done before, making a curious eager smouldering in her chest﻿—as Mrs. Corrie talked. An old sketchbook was brought out and Mrs. Corrie spent the morning making drawings of the heads of the children as they sat at lessons. The book was almost full of drawings of the children’s heads. Besides the heads there were rough sketches of people Miriam did not know. The first half-dozen pages were covered with small outlines, hands, feet, eyes, thumbs; a few lines suggesting a body. These pages seemed full of life. But the sketches of the children and the unknown people, sitting posed, in profile, looking up, looking down, full face, quarter face, three-quarters, depressed her. Learning to draw did not seem worth while if this was the result. The early pages haunted her memory as she sat over the children’s lessons. Feet, strange things stepping out, going through the world, running, dancing; the silent feet of people sitting in chairs pondering affairs of state. Eyes, looking at everything; looking at the astonishingness of everything.

===Chinese===
第二天，科里太太拿定主意不要那些帽子了。她要把那笔帽子钱花在素描课上。要请一位画家每星期来一趟，教他们全体对着自然写生。这个决定让米丽亚姆深深兴奋起来，把别的什么都从脑子里赶了出去。它应许了一桩心愿的满足——从学生时代起，她就怀着这桩心愿，怀着苦涩的无望；那时候，每个星期五都逼着她把渴望死死咽下去，渴望跟着那一小群修“额外课”的姑娘，懒懒散散地鱼贯而入，在画架与石膏像之间、那间大屋子里，度过一个有魔力的下午。旧日的渴望一跃而回，蹿得比以往哪一回都高，在她胸口燃起一股奇特的、急切的闷火——就在科里太太说话的当儿。一本旧写生簿翻出来了，科里太太花了一早上描孩子们的头像，他们正坐着做功课。簿子几乎画满了孩子们头部的素描。头像之外，还有些米丽亚姆不认识的人的草草速写。头几页涂满了小小的轮廓——手、脚、眼睛、拇指；几道线暗示出一个人形。这几页仿佛满是生命。可孩子们和那些陌生人的素描——摆好姿势坐着的、侧面的、仰头的、低头的、正脸的、四分脸的、四分之三的——却叫她沮丧。学画如果画成这样，似乎就不值当了。她坐在孩子们的功课旁边，最初那几页一直在她记忆里萦绕。脚，跨步出来的奇怪东西，穿行世界，奔跑，舞蹈；人们坐在椅子里运筹国事时那一双双安静的脚。眼睛，望着一切；望着一切令人惊叹的本相。

---

===Original===
“That’s the half-crown Mrs. Corrie gave me for the cabman, and the shilling for my tea,” said Miriam, handing the coins to her companion as they bowled over Waterloo Bridge. Seagulls were rising and dipping about the rim of the bridge and the sunlight lay upon the water and shimmered and flashed along the forms of the seagulls as they hovered and wheeled in the clear air. Miriam glanced at them through the little side window of the hansom with a remote keen part of her consciousness﻿ ﻿… light flashing from the moving wing of a seagull, the blue water, the brilliant sky, the bite of sun-scorched air upon her cheek, the sound about her like the sound of the sea.﻿ ﻿… As she turned back to the shaded enclosure of the hansom these things shrivelled and vanished and left her dumb, helplessly poised between two worlds. This shabby part of London and the seaside bridge could make no terms with the man at her side, his soft grey suit, his soft grey felt hat, the graceful crook of his crossed knees, his gleaming spats, the glitter of the light upon patent leather shoes. He was gazing out ahead, with the look with which he had looked across Australia in his gold-digging days, weary until he got back to the West End, not talking because the cab made such a noise crossing the bridge. It was stupid of her to peer out of her window and get away to her own world like that. Nothing that we can ever say to each other can possibly interest us, she reflected. Why am I here? Her coins reassured her.

“Don’t think about pence, dear girl,” he said, in a voice that quavered a little against the noise of the cab, “when you’re with old Bob.” Without looking at her he gently closed her hand over the money.

“All right,” she shouted, “we’ll see, later on!”

The cab swept round into a street and the noise abated.

===Chinese===
“这是科里太太给我打发车夫的半克朗，还有我那份茶点的一先令，”米丽亚姆说，把硬币递给同伴——马车正一路轻快地驶过滑铁卢桥（Waterloo Bridge）。海鸥绕着桥栏起起落落，阳光铺在水面上，沿着海鸥盘旋回翔的身形闪闪烁烁。米丽亚姆从双轮马车的小侧窗里望着它们，用的是她意识里一段遥远而锐利的部分……一道光从海鸥扇动的翅膀上闪过，蓝色的水，亮得晃眼的天，晒热的空气咬着她的脸颊，四周的声响像海的声音。……等她转回到双轮马车这方遮着凉的斗室里来，这些东西便蔫了、没了，撇下她说不出话，无助地悬在两个世界之间。伦敦这一带的寒酸街景、这座带着海滨气息的桥，同她身旁这位先生谈不拢——他那身柔软的灰西装，他那顶柔软的灰毡帽，他交叠的双膝那优雅的弯，他鞋口亮闪闪的鞋罩，光在他漆皮鞋上的闪烁。他朝前方凝望，带着他淘金年代横越澳大利亚时的那种神情，蔫蔫地挨着，直到回到西区才活过来；他不说话，因为过桥时车轮吵得厉害。她太蠢了，不该从小窗里探望出去，那样溜进自己的世界。我们彼此间能说出口的任何话，都不可能让我们俩任何一个感兴趣，她思量道。我为什么在这里？她那几枚硬币让她安下心来。

“别把便士放在心上，亲爱的姑娘，”他说，嗓音顶着马车的喧响微微发颤，“当你同老鲍勃在一起的时候。”他看也不看她，轻轻把她的手指合拢在那些钱上。

“好嘛，”她喊道，“回头见分晓！”

马车一拐扫进一条街，喧响退了下去。

===Original===
“When we’ve dropped those famous hats and rung the bell and run away we’ll go on to Bumpus’s and choose our book,” he said, as if asserting themselves and their errand against the confusion through which they were driving.

“Mrs. Caudle’s *Curtain Lectures*,” thought Miriam, glancing with loathing at the pointed corner of the collar that stuck out across the three firm little folds under the clean-shaven chin.﻿ ﻿… How funny I am. I suppose I shall get through the afternoon somehow. We shall go to the bookshop and then have tea and then it will be time to go back.

“The cabman is to take the hats into the shop and leave them. Isn’t it extraordinary?”

Bob laughed with a little fling of his head.

“The vagaries of the Fair, dear girl,” he said presently, in a soft blurred tone.

That’s one of his phrases, thought Miriam﻿—that’s old-fashioned politeness; courtliness. Behind it he’s got some sort of mannish thought﻿ ﻿… “the unaccountability of women”﻿ ﻿… who can understand a woman﻿—she doesn’t even understand herself﻿—thought he’d given up trying to make out. He’s gone through life and got his own impressions; all utterly wrong﻿ ﻿… talking about them with an air of wisdom to young men like Gerald﻿ ﻿… my dear boy, a woman never knows her own mind. How utterly detestable mannishness is; so mighty and strong and comforting when you have been mewed up with women all your life, and then suddenly, in a second, far away, utterly imbecile and aggravating with a superior self-satisfied smile because a woman says one thing one minute and another the next. Men ought to be horsewhipped, all the grown men, all who have ever had that self-satisfied smile, all, all, horsewhipped until they apologise on their knees.

===Chinese===
“等咱们把那几顶大名鼎鼎的帽子卸掉、按过铃、跑开之后，就接着上邦珀斯书店（Bumpus's）去挑咱们的书，”他说，仿佛要拿他们自己和他们这趟差事，来镇一镇他们正穿行其中的那一片纷乱。

“《考德尔太太的帐中训话》，”米丽亚姆想，厌恶地瞟了一眼那挺出的领尖——它斜斜叉过刮得干干净净的下巴底下三道结实的小褶。……我这人真好笑。我想这个下午我总归能对付过去。我们会去书店，然后吃茶点，然后就到该回去的时候了。

“车夫要把那些帽子搬进店里，放下就走。你说怪不怪？”

鲍勃笑了，头轻轻往上一扬。

“女人家的性子没个准儿（the vagaries of the Fair），亲爱的姑娘，”他过了一会儿说，嗓音软软的、含含糊糊。

这是他的口头禅之一，米丽亚姆想——那是旧式的客气；是骑士风度。它背后，他藏着某一种男人气的想法……“女人的不可索解”……谁能懂一个女人——她连自己都不懂——想着他已经死了探究这条心。他走完了一辈子人生，攥下了自己的一堆印象；全错得离谱……还要带着一脸智慧，讲给杰拉尔德这样的年轻人听……我亲爱的孩子啊，女人从来不知道自己要什么。男人气这东西多么彻头彻尾地可憎；你一辈子跟女人们关在一处，它显得那么雄壮、那么强大、那么可依靠；可忽然之间，一秒钟，它退到远远的，蠢透了、气死人，还挂着一副优越的、自满的笑——就因为女人这一分钟说这样、下一分钟说那样。男人们理应吃马鞭，所有成年的男人，所有露出过那副自满的笑的，一个个，一个个，抽到他们跪下赔罪为止。

---

===Original===
They sat in a curious oak settee, like a high-backed church pew. The waitress had cleared away the tea things and brought cigarettes, large flat Turkish cigarettes. Responding to her companion’s elaborate apologetic petition for permission to smoke it did not occur to Miriam to confess that she herself occasionally smoked. She forgot the fact in the completeness of her contentment. On the square oak table in front of them was a bowl of garden anemones, mauve and scarlet with black centres, flaring richly in the soft light coming through the green-tinted diamond panes of a little low square deep-silled window. On either side of the window short red curtains were drawn back and hung in straight, close folds﻿ ﻿… scarlet geraniums﻿ ﻿… against the creamy plaster wall. Bowls of flowers stood on other tables placed without crowding or confusion about the room and there was another green window with red curtains near a far-off corner. There were no other customers for the greater part of their time and when the waitress was not in the room it was still; a softly shaded stillness. Bob’s low blurred voice had gone on and on undisturbingly, no questions about her life or her plans, just jokes, about the tea-service and everything they had had, making her laugh. Whenever she laughed, he laughed delightedly. All the time her eyes had wandered from the brilliant anemones across to the soft green window with its scarlet curtains.

===Chinese===
他们坐在一张古怪的橡木高背长椅里，像教堂里一排高背的座席。女侍者收走了茶具，端来了香烟，又大又扁的土耳其烟。同伴客客气气、一迭声赔着不是地请求准许吸烟，她应承着，却压根没想到招认自己偶尔也抽。她把这一茬忘了个干净，忘在她那份圆满的满足里。他们面前的方橡木桌上摆着一碗园子里的银莲花，紫红的、猩红的，黑黑的花心，在柔光里开得浓浓的——光从一扇小小的、低低的、方方的、窗台深深的绿菱形格小窗透进来。窗子两侧，短短的红帘挽起，垂成一道道笔直的密褶……猩红的天竺葵……衬着奶色的灰泥墙。一碗碗鲜花立在别的桌上，满屋子摆得从容不乱，远远的角落近旁还有又一扇挂红帘的绿窗。大半个时段里没有别的客人，女侍者不在屋里时，一片静；一种蒙着软影的静。鲍勃那低低的、含混的嗓音絮絮地说着，一直说下去，不搅扰人，不问她的生活，也不问她的打算，只说笑话，说茶具，说他们用过的每一样东西，逗她笑。每逢她一笑，他便欢喜地跟着笑。自始至终，她的眼睛从那些明艳的银莲花，游过去，游到那扇柔和的、挂着猩红帘子的绿窗上。

## VIII / VIII

===Original===
When May came life lay round Miriam without a flaw. She seemed to have reached the summit of a hill up which she had been climbing ever since she came to Newlands. The weeks had been green lanes of experience, fresh and scented and balmy and free from lurking fears. Now the landscape lay open before her eyes, clear from horizon to horizon, sunlit and flawless, past and future. The present, within her hands, brought her, whenever she paused to consider it, to the tips of her toes, as if its pressure lifted her. She would push it off, smiling﻿—turning and shutting herself away from it, with laughter and closed eyes, she found herself deeper in the airy flood and drawing breath swam forward.
The old troubles, the things she had known from the beginning, the general shadow that lay over the family life and closed punctually in whenever the sun began to shine, her own personal thoughts, the impossibility of living with people, poverty, disease, death in a dark corner, had moved and changed, melted and flowed away.
The family shadow had shrunk long ago, back in the winter months they had spent in Bennett’s little bachelor villa, to a small black cloud of disgrace hanging over her father. At the time of its appearance, when the extent of his embarrassment was exactly known, she had sunk for a while under the conviction that the rest of her life must be spent in a vain attempt to pay off his debts. Her mind revolved round the problem hopelessly.﻿ ﻿… Even if she went on the stage she could not make enough to pay off one of his creditors. Most women who went on the stage, Gerald had said, made practically nothing, and the successful ones had to spend enormous sums in bribery whilst they were making their way﻿—even the orchestra expected to be flattered and bribed. She would have to go on being a resident governess, keeping ten pounds a year for dress and paying over the rest of her salary. Her bitter rebellion against this prospect was reinforced by the creditors’ refusal to make her father a bankrupt. The refusal brought her a picture of the creditors, men “on the Stock Exchange,” sitting in a circle, in frock-coats, talking over her father’s affairs. She winced, her blood came scorching against her skin. She confronted them, “Stop!” she shouted, “stop *talking*﻿—you smug ugly men! You shall be paid. Stop! Go away.﻿ ﻿…” But Gerald had said, “They *like* the old boy﻿ ﻿… it won’t hurt *them*﻿ ﻿… they’re all made of money.” They liked him. They would be kind. What right had they to be “kind”? They would be kind to her too. They would smile at her plan of restitution and put it on one side. And yet secretly she knew that each one of them would like to be paid and was vexed and angry at losing money just as she was angry at having to sacrifice her life to them. She would not sacrifice her life, but if ever she found herself wealthy she would find out their names and pay them secretly. Probably that would be never.

===Chinese===
五月一到，生活环绕着米丽亚姆，再无一丝瑕疵。她仿佛已经登上了一座山顶——自从来到纽兰兹，她便一直在向着它攀登。这几个星期是一条条青翠的经验之巷，新鲜、芬芳、温煦，没有潜伏暗处的恐惧。此刻风景在她眼前铺展开来，从天际到天际一片澄明，阳光普照，无瑕无垢，过去与未来皆是。而现在，就在她双手之间，每当她停下来凝想它，总把她托上脚尖，仿佛它的压力把她轻轻托起。她会笑着把它推开——转过身，带着笑、阖着眼把自己从它面前关起来，却发现自己已更深地陷进这空灵的洪流，便吸一口气，向前游去。

旧的烦恼，那些她从一开始便认得的东西——罩在家庭生活上、每当太阳刚一露脸便准时围拢过来的那片笼统的阴影——她自己的种种私念，与人共同生活之不可能，贫穷、疾病、躲在暗角落里的死亡——都已挪动、变了样，融化、流走了。

家庭的阴影早已缩小了——还是回到他们在贝内特那幢独身小别墅过冬的那几个月里——缩成了悬在她父亲头顶的一小朵乌黑耻辱之云。它初初现形的时候，他那窘迫的底细一经查明，她曾一度沉下去，确信自己的余生注定要耗在还清他债务的徒劳尝试里。她的心思绝望地绕着这个难题打转。……就算她登台演戏，也挣不够还他一位债主的数。杰拉尔德（Gerald）说过，登台的女人大多数实际上一文挣不着，混出头的那些，还得在往上爬的路上花大笔大笔的钱打点行贿——连乐队的乐师都指望受人奉承、得些好处。她就只好继续做住家家庭教师，一年留十镑置装，其余的薪水尽数缴出去。债权人们拒绝让她父亲宣告破产，越发加固了她对这种前途的苦涩反叛。这一拒绝送给她一幅图景：那些债权人，“交易所”上的先生们，围坐成一圈，穿着长礼服（frock-coats），议论着她父亲的事务。她浑身一缩，血灼灼地烫上皮肤。她迎面顶住他们。“住口！”她喊道，“别再*议论*了——你们这些道貌岸然的丑男人！会还给你们的。住口！走开。……”可杰拉尔德说过：“他们*喜欢*这老家伙……又亏不着*他们*……他们个个都是钱堆出来的。”他们喜欢他。他们会发善心。他们有什么权利发“善心”？他们对她也会发善心。他们会朝她的偿还计划付之一笑，把它搁到一边。可她私下里知道，他们每一个都巴不得拿到钱，都在蚀本时又懊又恼，正如她为不得不为他们牺牲自己的生命而恼恨。她不会牺牲自己的生命；可有朝一日她若发了财，她会查出他们的名字，悄悄把钱还清。多半那一天永远不会来。

===Original===
Disgrace closed round her, stifling. “It’s *us*﻿—we’re doomed,” she thought, feeling the stigma of her family in her flesh. “If I go on after this, holding up my head, I shall be a liar and a cheat. It will show in my face and in my walk, always.” She bowed her head. “I want to live,” murmured something. “I want to live, even if I slink through life. I will. I don’t care inside. I shall always have myself to be with.”
Something that was not touched, that sang far away down inside the gloom, that cared nothing for the creditors and could get away down and down into the twilight far away from the everlasting accusations of humanity.﻿ ﻿… The disgrace sat only in the muscles of her face, in her muscles, the stuff of her that had defied and fought and been laughed at and beaten. It would not get deeper. Deeper down was something cool and fresh﻿—endless﻿—an endless garden. In happiness it came up and made everything in the world into a garden. Sorrow blotted it over, but it was always there, waiting and looking on. It had looked on in Germany and had loved the music and the words and the happiness of the German girls and at Banbury Park, giving her no peace until she got away.
And now it had come to the surface and was with her all the time. Away in the distance filling in the horizon was the home life. Beyond the horizon, gone away forever into some outer darkness were her old ideas of trouble, disease and death. Once they had been always quite near at hand, always ready to strike, laying cold hands on everything. They would return, but they would be changed. No need to fear them any more. She had seen them change. And when at last they came back, when there was nothing else left in front of her they would still be changing. “Get along, old ghosts,” she said, and they seemed friendly and smiling. Her father and mother, whose failure and death she had foreseen as a child with sudden bitter tears, were going on now step by step towards these ghostly things in the small bright lamplit villa in Gunnersbury. She had watched them there during the winter months before she came to Newlands. They had some secret together and did not feel the darkness. Their eyes were careless and bright. Startled, she had heard them laugh together as they talked in their room. Often their eyes were preoccupied, as if they were looking at a picture. She had laughed aloud at the thought whenever there had been any excuse, and they had always looked at her when she laughed her loud laugh. Had they understood? Did they know that it was themselves laughing in her? Families ought to laugh together whenever there was any excuse. She felt that her own grown-up laughter was the end of all the dreadful years. And three weeks ahead were the two weddings. The letters from home gleamed with descriptions of the increasing store of presents and new-made clothing. Miriam felt that they were her own; she would see them all at the last best moment when they were complete. She would have all that and all her pride in the outgoing lives of Sarah and Harriett that were like two sunlit streams. And meanwhile here within her hands was Newlands. Three weeks of days and nights of untroubled beauty. Interminable.

===Chinese===
耻辱围拢过来，闷得人透不过气。“是*我们*——我们完了。”她想，感到自家的污名烙在皮肉里。“这以后我若还昂着头走下去，我就是个说谎的、行骗的。它会显在我脸上、显在我走路的样子里，永远。”她垂下头。“我要活。”有什么东西喃喃着。“我要活，哪怕我偷偷摸摸地溜过这一生。我就是要活。我内里并不在乎。我永远有我自己做伴。”

有一样东西是没有被触到的，它在幽暗深处远远地歌唱，全然不把债权人放在心上，能够一径往下去、再下去，沉入暮色，远远离开人类那没完没了的指控。……耻辱只盘踞在她脸部的肌肉里，在她的肌肉里，在那反抗过、争斗过、被嘲笑过也被击打过的她的质料里。它不会再深进去。更深的地方有一样清凉而新鲜的东西——无穷无尽——一座无穷无尽的花园。幸福时它便浮上来，把世上的一切都变成花园。哀伤把它涂抹遮没，可它永远在那里，等着，望着。在德国它曾望着，爱过那音乐、那些词句、那些德国姑娘的幸福；在班伯里园它也曾望着，害得她不得安生，直到她脱身走开。

而今它已浮上表面，时时刻刻同她在一起。远处填满天际的，是家里的生活。天际之外，永远隐没进某种外层黑暗里去的，是她关于烦恼、疾病与死亡的旧观念。它们一度总在近旁，随时准备出击，把冰冷的手搭上一切。它们还会回来，但将会变了样。再不必怕它们了。她已经见过它们变化。而等它们终于回来、她面前再没有别的东西的时候，它们也仍将变化着。“去吧去吧，老幽灵。”她说，它们显得又友善、又含着笑。她的父亲和母亲——她小时候曾带着突然涌上的苦泪，预见过他们的败落与死亡——如今正在冈纳斯伯里那幢灯火明亮的小小别墅里，一步一步走向这些幽灵似的东西。来纽兰兹之前的那个冬天，她曾在那里望着他们。他们俩之间有一个共同的秘密，感觉不到黑暗。他们的眼睛无忧无虑、亮亮的。她曾吃了一惊，听见他们在自己房间里说着话、一同笑起来。他们的眼睛常常是若有所思的，仿佛正望着一幅画。每逢有个由头，她就为这个念头放声大笑，而每逢她放声大笑，他们总要朝她望过来。他们懂了么？他们可知道，在她身上笑着的，正是他们自己？一家人只要有个由头，原就该一同大笑。她觉得，她自己这长大成人的笑，是一切可怕年月的终结。再过三个星期，就是那两场婚礼。家里的来信亮闪闪地写满了越积越多的赠礼、一件件新做的衣裳。米丽亚姆觉得它们都是她自己的；她会在它们圆满齐全的最后最美好的时刻，把它们全都看到。她将拥有这一切，连同她那份对萨拉与哈丽特的骄傲——她们那两条洒满阳光的溪流般流出去的生命。而与此同时，就在她双手之间的，是纽兰兹。三个星期的、无忧无虑的美丽的日日夜夜。没有尽头。

---

===Original===
The roses were in bud. Every day she managed to visit them at least once, running out alone into the garden at twilight and coming back rich with the sense of the twilit green garden and the increasing stripes of colour between the tight shining green sheaths.

===Chinese===
玫瑰都打苞了。每天她总要设法去看它们至少一回，独自在暮色里跑进花园，回来时满载着那暮色中青翠花园的感觉，还有紧绷绷、亮闪闪的绿鞘之间日渐增多的一道道色彩。

---

===Original===
There had been no more talk of painting lessons. The idea had died in Mrs. Corrie’s mind the day after it had been born and a strange interest, something dreadful that was happening in London had taken its place. It seemed to absorb her completely and to spread a strange curious excitement throughout the house. She sent a servant every afternoon up to the station for an evening newspaper. The pink papers disappeared, but she was perpetually making allusions to their strange secret in a way that told Miriam she wanted to impart it and that irritated without really arousing her interest. She felt that anything that was being fussed over in pink evening papers was probably really nothing at all. She could not believe that anything that had such a strange effect on Mrs. Corrie could really interest her. But she longed to know exactly what the mysterious thing was. If it was simply a divorce case Mrs. Corrie would have told her about it, dropping out the whole story abstractedly in one of her little shocked sentences and immediately going on to speak of something else. She did not want to hear anything more about divorce; all her interested curiosity in divorced people had been dispersed by her contact with the Kronens. They had both been divorced and their lives were broken and muddly and they were not sure of themselves. Mrs. Kronen was strong and alone. But she was alone and would always be. If it were a murder everybody would talk about it openly. It must be something worse than a murder or a divorce. She felt she must know, must make Mrs. Corrie tell her and knew at the same time that she did not want to be distracted from the pure solid glory of the weeks by sharing a horrible secret. The thing kept Mrs. Corrie occupied and interested and left her free to live undisturbed. It was a barrier between them. And yet﻿ ﻿… something that a human being had done that was worse than a murder or a divorce.

===Chinese===
绘画课再没有被提起过。这念头在科里太太心里生下来的第二天就死了，顶替它位置的，是一桩新奇的兴味——伦敦正在出着的一件可怕的事。它似乎把她整个人吸了进去，又在整座宅子里撒开一片古怪的好奇与兴奋。每天下午她都打发一个仆人到车站去买一份晚报。那些粉红报纸（the pink papers）转眼便不见了踪影，可她没完没了地影射着他们那个奇怪的秘密，那副样子告诉米丽亚姆，她一心想把它说出来，叫人恼火，却又实在勾不起她的兴趣。她觉得，凡是粉红晚报上这样小题大做的，多半其实什么也不是。她无法相信，一件对科里太太有这么奇怪影响的事，真能让她感兴趣。可她又渴望确切知道那桩神秘的事到底是什么。倘若不过是一桩离婚案，科里太太早就告诉她了——会心不在焉地用她那套吃惊的小句子把整个故事零零碎碎抖出来，紧接着就去说别的。她不想再听任何离婚的事；她对离过婚的人那点津津有味的好奇，早被同克罗嫩夫妇的来往打散了。他们俩都离过婚，他们的生活是破碎的、乱糟糟的，他们对自己也没有把握。克罗嫩太太坚强，也孤单。可她终究孤单，还会一直孤单下去。倘若是一桩凶杀，人人都会公开谈论。那一定是比凶杀或离婚更坏的事。她觉得自己必须知道，必须让科里太太说出来，可同时又明白，她并不想因为分享一个可怕的秘密，就从这几个星期纯粹而坚实的荣光里分神。这件事让科里太太有事可忙、兴味十足，也由得她自由自在、不受搅扰地过活。它是横在她们中间的一道屏障。然而……一个人做出来的事，竟比凶杀或离婚更坏。

===Original===
“Is it a divorce?” she said suddenly and insincerely one afternoon coming upon Mrs. Corrie scanning the newly arrived newspaper in the garden.
“Lordy no,” laughed Mrs. Corrie self-consciously, scrumpling the paper under her arm.
“What is it?” said Miriam, shaking and flushing. “Don’t tell me, don’t tell me,” cried her mind, “don’t mention it, you don’t know yourself what it is. Nobody knows what anything is.”
“I couldn’t tell you!” cried Mrs. Corrie.

===Chinese===
一天下午，她在花园里撞见科里太太正细看刚送到的报纸，便突兀地、言不由衷地问：“是离婚案么？”

“哎呀，才不是呢。”科里太太不自然地笑着，把报纸揉作一团掖到胳膊底下。

“那是什么呀？”米丽亚姆说，浑身发颤，脸也红了。“别告诉我，别告诉我。”她的心喊着，“别提起它，你自己也不知道它是什么。没有谁知道任何东西是什么。”

“我可说不得呀！”科里太太喊道。

===Original===
“Why *not*?” laughed Miriam.
“It’s too awful,” giggled Mrs. Corrie.
“Oh, you must tell me now you’ve begun.”
“It’s the most awful thing there is. It’s like the Bible,” said Mrs. Corrie, and fled into the house.

===Chinese===
“为什么*说不得*？”米丽亚姆笑了。

“太可怕了呀。”科里太太咯咯地笑。

“哦，都开了头啦，你一定得告诉我。”

“那是天底下顶顶可怕的事。简直像《圣经》里的事。”科里太太说着，逃进屋里去了。

---

===Original===
Little cities burning and flaring in a great plain until everything was consumed. Everything beginning again﻿—clean. Would London be visited by destruction? Humanity was as bad now as in Bible days. It made one feel cold and sick. In the midst of the beauty and happiness of England﻿—awful things, the worst things there were. What awful faces those people must have. It would be dreadful to see them.

===Chinese===
小小的城池在大平原上焚烧、腾焰，直到一切烧尽。一切重新开始——干干净净。毁灭也会临到伦敦么？如今的人类和《圣经》时代一样坏。这让人发冷、发呕。就在英格兰的美与幸福正当中——可怕的东西，顶顶坏的东西。那些人必定长着多么可怕的脸。见到他们将是可怕的。

---

===Original===
At the weekend the house seemed full of little groups of conspirators, talking in corners, full of secret glee﻿ ﻿… someone describing a room, drawn curtains and candlelight at midday﻿ ﻿… wonderful﻿ ﻿… and laughing. Why did they laugh? A candlelit room in the midst of bright day﻿ ﻿… wonderful, like a shrine.
The low-toned talk went on, in Mr. Corrie’s little study behind the half-closed door, in corners of the hall. Names were mentioned﻿—the name of the man who wrote the plays, Mrs. Kronen’s “genius.” Miriam could only recall when she was alone that it was a woodland springtime name. It comforted her to think that this name was concerned in the horrible mystery. Her sympathies veered vaguely out towards the patch of disgrace in London and her interest died down.

===Chinese===
到了周末，宅子里仿佛满是三三两两的同谋者，在角落里低语，满肚子偷偷的欢喜……有人在描述一个房间，拉上的窗帘，白昼正中点着的烛光……妙啊……还笑着。他们为什么笑？光天化日正当中一间烛光的房间……妙啊，像一座神龛。

压低嗓音的谈话继续着，在科里先生那间小书房里、半掩的门后，在门厅的角落里。有人提到一些名字——写那些戏的那人的名字，克罗嫩太太的“天才”。米丽亚姆只有在独自一人时才能记起，那是个属于林间春光的名字。想到这个名字也牵涉在这可怕的神秘里，她反而觉得安心。她的同情隐隐约约地转向伦敦那一小块耻辱，她的兴味便渐渐熄下去了。

---

===Original===
The general preoccupation and excitement seemed to destroy her link with the household. As soon as the children’s tea was over she felt herself free. A strange tall woman came to stay in the house, trailing about in long jewelled dresses with a slight limp; Miss Tower, Mrs. Corrie called her Jin. But the name did not belong to her. Miriam could not think of any name that would belong to her﻿ ﻿… talking to Mrs. Corrie at lunch with amused eyes and expressionless, small fine features of some illness that was going to kill her in eight or ten years, of her friends, talking about her men friends as if they were boys to be cried over. “Why don’t you marry him?” Mrs. Corrie would say of one or another. How happy the man would be, thought Miriam, gazing into the strange eyes and daring her to marry anyone and alter the eyes. Miss Tower spoke to her now and again as if she had known her all her life. One day after lunch she suddenly said, “You ought to smile more often﻿—you’ve got pretty teeth; but you forget about them. Don’t forget about them”; and one evening she came into her room just as she was beginning to undress and stood by the fire and said, “Your evening dresses are all wrong. You should have them cut higher, above the collarbone﻿—or much lower﻿—don’t forget. Don’t forget, you could be charming.”
Mrs. Corrie came in herself the next evening and gave Miriam a full-length cabinet photograph of herself, suddenly. Afterwards she heard her saying to Kate on the landing, “Let the poor thing rest when she can,” and they both went into Kate’s room.

===Chinese===
人人那份放不下的心事和兴奋，仿佛毁掉了她同这个家的联结。孩子们的茶点一吃完，她就觉得自己自由了。一个高大而陌生的女人来家里做客，拖着微跛的步子，曳着缀满珠玉的长裙四处走动；塔沃尔小姐（Miss Tower）——科里太太唤她金（Jin）。可这名字不属于她。米丽亚姆想不出有哪个名字能属于她……午饭时她同科里太太谈着话，含着笑意的眼睛，配上毫无表情的、小巧精致的五官，谈着某种再过八到十年便要夺去她性命的病，谈着她的朋友们，谈起她的男朋友们来，仿佛他们是一群让人揪心落泪的男孩。“你怎么不嫁给他呢？”这一个、那一个，科里太太总要这样说。那男人该多幸福啊，米丽亚姆想，一面凝望进那双奇异的眼睛，一面在心里激她——倒嫁个人试试，改一改这双眼睛。塔沃尔小姐时不时同她说说话，仿佛已经认识了她一辈子。有一天午饭过后她突然说：“你该多多地笑——你有一口漂亮的牙齿；可你总忘了它们。别忘了它们”；一天晚上，她正开始宽衣，她走进她房间，站在炉边说：“你的晚礼服全都不对。该让它们裁得高些，高过锁骨——或者低得多——别忘了。别忘了，你可以很迷人的。”

第二天傍晚，科里太太竟亲自进了她的房间，突然送给米丽亚姆一张她自己的全身照片（cabinet photograph）。随后她听见她在楼梯平台上对凯特（Kate）说：“能让这可怜的人儿歇的时候就让她歇歇吧。”接着她们俩进了凯特的房间。

---

===Original===
Every day as soon as the children’s tea was over she fled to her room. The memory of Mrs. Corrie’s little sketchbook had haunted her for days. She had bought a block and brushes, a small box of paints and a book on painting in water colours. For days she painted, secure in the feeling of Mrs. Corrie and Kate occupied with each other. She filled sheet after sheet with swift efforts to recall Brighton skies﻿—sunset, the red mass of the sun, the profile of the cliffs, the sky clear or full of heavy cloud, the darkness of the afternoon sea streaked by a path of gold, bird-specks, above the cliffs, above the sea. The painting was thick and confused, the objects blurred and ran into each other, the image of each recalled object came close before her eyes, shaking her with its sharp reality, her heart and hand shook as she contemplated it, and her body thrilled as she swept her brushes about. She found herself breathing heavily and deeply, sure each time of registering what she saw, sweeping rapidly on until the filled paper confronted her, a confused mass of shapeless images, leaving her angry and cold. Each day what she had done the day before thrilled her afresh and drove her on, and the time she spent in contemplation and hope became the heart of the days as April wore on.

===Chinese===
每天，孩子们的茶点一结束，她就逃回自己房间。科里太太那本小小写生簿的记忆，一连好几天缠着她。她买了一本画纸本（block）、几支画笔、一小盒颜料和一本讲水彩画的书。一连多日她画着画，心里安稳，知道科里太太和凯特正彼此忙着彼此。她一张接一张地画满画纸，飞快地试着追回布莱顿（Brighton）的天空——落日，太阳那红彤彤的一团，断崖的侧影，或澄澈或堆满重云的天，午后海面的幽暗被一条金色的小径划开，鸟雀的墨点点，在断崖之上，在大海之上。画出来的东西又厚又乱，物象模糊洇散、彼此渗透，每一个追回的物象的影像直逼到她眼前，用它锋利的真实撼着她，她凝视它时，心和手都在抖，她挥动画笔时，浑身战栗。她发觉自己呼吸又重又深，每一次都确信这回总算把她看见的记了下来，飞快地一路画下去，直到画满的纸迎面对着她——一团乱糟糟、不成形状的影像——留下她又恼又冷。每一天，前一天画下的东西都重新让她激动、催着她往前，而她花在凝望与盼望上的光阴，随着四月一天天过去，成了这些日子的心脏。

---

===Original===
On the last day of Jin Tower’s visit, Miriam came in from the garden upon Mrs. Corrie sitting in the hall with her guest. Jin was going and was sorry that she was going. But Miriam saw that her gladness was as great as her sorrow. It always would be. Whatever happened to her. Mrs. Corrie was sitting at her side bent from the waist with her arms stretched out and hands clasped beyond her knees. Miriam was amazed to see how much Mrs. Corrie had been talking, and that she was treating Jin’s departure as if it were a small crisis. There was a touch of soft heat and fussiness in the air. Mrs. Corrie’s features were discomposed. They both glanced at her as she came across the hall and she smiled, awkwardly and half paused. Her mind was turned towards her vision of a great cliff in profile against a still sky with a deep sea brimming to its feet in a placid afterglow; the garden with its lawn and trees, its bushiness and its buttons of bright rosebuds had seemed small and troubled and talkative in comparison. In her slight pause she offered them her vision, but knew as she went on upstairs that her attitude had said, “I am the paid governess. You must not talk to me as you would to each other; I am an inferior and can never be an intimate.” She was glad that Jin had left off coming to her room. She did not want intimacy with anyone if it meant that strained fussiness in the hall. Meeting Mrs. Corrie later on the landing she asked with a sudden sense of inspiration whether she might have her meal in her room, adding in an insincere effort at explanation that she wanted to do some reading up for the children. Mrs. Corrie agreed with an alacrity that gave her a vision of possible freedom ahead and a shock of apprehension. Perhaps she had not succeeded even so far as she thought in living the Newlands social life. She spent the evening writing to Eve, asking her if she remembered sea scenes at Weymouth and Brighton, pushing on and on weighed down by a sense of the urgency of finding out whether to Eve the registration and the recalling of her impressions was a thing that she must either do or lose hold of some essential thing﻿ ﻿… she felt that Eve would somehow admire her own stormy emphasis but would not really understand how much it meant to her. She remembered Eve’s comparison of the country round the Greens’ house to Leader landscapes﻿—pictures, and how delightful it had seemed to her that she had such things all round her to look at. But her thoughts of the great brow and downward sweep of cliff and the sea coming up to it was not a picture, it was a thing; her cheeks flared as she searched for a word﻿—it was an experience, perhaps the most important thing in life﻿—far in away from any “glad mask,” a thing belonging to that strange inner life and independent of everybody. Perhaps it was a betrayal, a sort of fat noisy gossiping to speak of it even to Eve. “You’ll think I’m *mad*,” she concluded, “but I’m not.”

===Chinese===
金·塔沃尔做客的最后一天，米丽亚姆从花园进来，正撞见科里太太陪着她的客人坐在门厅里。金要走了，也为自己的走难过。可米丽亚姆看出，她的欢喜和她的难过一样大。永远会是这样。无论她后来遭遇到什么。科里太太坐在她身旁，从腰际起向前弯着，两条胳膊伸出去，手在膝盖外头交扣着。米丽亚姆惊异地看到科里太太竟说了这么多话，还看到她把金的离去当作一场小小的危机来对待。空气里有一丝软软的热气和忙乱。科里太太的五官乱了方寸。米丽亚姆穿过门厅时，两人都朝她瞥了一眼，她笑了笑，笨拙地把脚步顿了半顿。她的心思正转向她的幻景——一堵巨大的断崖，侧影衬着一动不动的天空，深深的海水平盈盈地漫到它脚下，浮在一片安恬的余晖里；相比之下，这座有草坪有树木、蓬蓬簇簇、缀满一粒粒亮玫瑰花蕾的花园，便显得又小、又烦乱、又聒噪。在她那半顿的迟疑里，她把她的幻景向她们递了过去，可当她继续上楼时她知道，自己的姿态说的是：“我是拿薪水的家庭教师。你们不可以像彼此之间那样同我说话；我是低人一等的，永远成不了亲近的人。”她庆幸金不再来她的房间了。她不想同任何人亲近，倘若亲近意味着门厅里那种绷紧的忙乱的话。后来在楼梯平台上遇见科里太太，她凭一阵突至的灵机，问她能否把饭开在自己房间里，又言不由衷地找补了一句，说她想给孩子们预先读些书。科里太太答应得那样爽快——爽快得让她瞥见前方可能的自由，也让她心头一惊、生出忧惧。也许，在过纽兰兹的社交生活这件事上，她远远没有做到自己以为做到的那一步。她把整个晚上用来给伊芙写信，问她记不记得韦茅斯和布莱顿的海景，一程一程往下赶，心头沉甸甸压着这件事的急迫——非得弄清楚不可：对伊芙来说，把她见到的景象记录下来、召唤回来，究竟是不是一件她非做不可、不做便要失手放过某种要紧东西的事……她觉得伊芙多半会欣赏她这狂风暴雨式的强调，却不会真正懂得这对她意味着多少。她想起伊芙曾把格林家四周的乡野比作利德（Leader）的风景画——一幅幅画；而她当时觉得，这样的东西四围皆是、尽她观看，多么叫人欢喜。可她心里那道巨崖的额头、那向下扫落的崖壁、那直漫上来的海，不是一幅画，是一件东西；她两颊灼灼地找一个词——那是一场经验，也许是生命里顶要紧的东西——远远退开一切“美面假面”（glad mask），一件属于那种奇异内在生活、独立于所有人的东西。也许说出来就是一种背叛，哪怕只说给伊芙，也是一种胖乎乎、闹哄哄的闲话。“你会以为我*疯*了，”她这样收尾，“可我没有疯。”

===Original===
When the letter was finished the Newlands life seemed very remote. She was alone in a strange, luxurious room that did not belong to her, lit by a hard electric light that had been put there by some hardworking mechanic to whom the house was just a house with electric fittings. She felt a touch of the half-numb half-feverish stupor that had been her daily mood at Banbury Park. She would go on teaching the Corrie children, but her evenings in future would be divided between unsuccessful efforts to put down her flaming or peaceful sunset scenes and to explain their importance to Eve.

===Chinese===
信号好了，纽兰兹的生活显得非常遥远。她独自在一间豪华而陌生的、不属于她的房间里，头顶亮着一道硬邦邦的电灯光——那灯是哪个卖力气的匠人装上去的，对那匠人来说，这宅子不过是一所装了电灯的宅子。她感到了一点那种半麻木、半发热的昏沉——在班伯里园，这曾是她天天的心境。她还要继续教科里家的孩子，可她往后的夜晚，将分成两半：一半用来徒劳地试着把她那些火焰般或宁静的落日景象落到纸面，一半用来向伊芙解释它们的重要性。

## IX / IX

===Original===
But the next evening when Mr. Corrie came down for the weekend with a party of guests, Mrs. Corrie appeared with swift suddenness in Miriam’s room and glanced at her morning dress.
“I say, missy, you’ll have to hurry up.”
“Oh, I didn’t dress﻿ ﻿… the house is full of strangers.”
“No, it isn’t; there’s Mélie and Tom﻿ ﻿… Tommy and Mélie.”
“Yes, but I know there are crowds.”
She did not want to meet the Cravens again, and the strangers would turn out to be some sort of people saying certain sorts of things over and over again, and if she went down she would not be able to get away as soon as she knew all about them. She would be fixed; obliged to listen. When anyone spoke to her, grimacing as the patronised governess or saying what she thought and being hated for it.

===Chinese===
可是第二天傍晚，科里先生带着一伙客人下来过周末，科里太太旋风似的突然出现在米丽亚姆房间里，朝她那身晨装瞥了一眼。

“我说，小姑娘，你可得赶紧着点儿。”

“哦，我没有换衣服……宅子里满是生人。”

“没有的事；有梅莉和汤姆……汤米和梅莉呢。”

“是，可我知道有一大群人。”

她不想再见到克雷文夫妇，而那些生人，到头来准是某一路的人物，把某几路的话颠来倒去地讲，她若是下了楼，等把他们的底细摸清了，也就脱不开身了。她会被钉在那里；不得不听。谁一开口同她讲话，她要么就得摆出受着人家恩遇的家庭教师的鬼脸，要么就说出心里所想、招人记恨。

===Original===
“Crowds,” she repeated, as Mrs. Corrie placed a large lump in the centre of the blaze.
They had her here, in this beautiful room and looked after her comfort as if she were a guest.
“Nonsensy-nonsense. You *must* come down and see the fun.” Miriam glanced at her empty table. In the drawer hidden underneath the table-cover were her block and paints. Presently she could, if she held firm, be alone, in a grey space inside this alien room, cold and lonely and with the beginning of something﻿ ﻿… dark painful beginning of something that could not come if people were there.﻿ ﻿… Downstairs, warmth and revelry.
“You *must* come down and see the fun,” said Mrs. Corrie, getting up from the fire and trailing across the room with bent head. “A nun﻿—a nun in amber satin,” thought Miriam, surveying her back.
“*Want* you to come down,” said Mrs. Corrie plaintively from the door. Cold air came in from the landing; the warmth of the room stirred to a strange vitality, the light glowed clearer within its ruby globe. The silvery clatter of entrée dishes came up from the hall.
“All right,” said Miriam, turning exultantly to the chest of drawers.

===Chinese===
“一大群。”她重复道，这时科里太太正往炉焰正中搁上一大块煤。

他们把她安置在这里，在这间美丽的房间里，照管着她的舒适，仿佛她是位客人。

“胡说－瞎说。你*一定*得下来看看热闹。”米丽亚姆朝自己那张空桌子瞥了一眼。桌布底下藏着的抽屉里，是她的画纸本和颜料。待一会儿，只要她顶得住，她就可以独自一人，在这间陌生房间当中的一个灰色空间里，又冷又孤单，还有某样东西的开端……一样黑暗的、痛苦的东西的开端——只要有人在跟前，它就不肯来。……楼下呢，是温暖，是欢宴。

“你*一定*得下来看看热闹。”科里太太说着，从炉边起身，垂着头拖沓着穿过房间。“一个修女——一个穿琥珀缎的修女。”米丽亚姆打量着她的背影，想。

“*要*你下来嘛。”科里太太在门口哀声说。冷空气从楼梯平台上漫进来；房里的暖意被搅动起一种奇异的生气，灯光在它那红宝石似的灯球里烧得更亮了。门厅里传上来主菜盘盏银亮的磕碰声。

“好吧。”米丽亚姆说，扬扬得意地转向那口五斗柜。

===Original===
“A victory over myself or some sort of treachery?”﻿ ﻿… The long drawer which held her evening things seemed full of wonders. She dragged out a little homemade smocked blouse of pale blue nun’s veiling that had seemed too dowdy for Newlands and put it on over her morning skirt. It shone upon her. Rapidly washing her hands, away from the glamour of the looking-glass, she mentally took stock of her hair, untouched since the morning, the amateur blouse, its crude clear blue hard against the harsh black skirt. Back again at the dressing-table as she dried her hands she found the miracle renewed. The figure that confronted her in the mirror was wrapped in some strange harmonising radiance. She looked at it for a moment as she would have looked at an unknown picture, in tranquil disinterested contemplation. The sound of the gong came softly into the room, bringing her no apprehensive contraction of nerves. She wove its lingering note into the imagined tinkling of an old melody from a wooden musical box. Opening the door before turning out her gas she found a small bunch of hothouse lilies of the valley lying on the writing-table.﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Corrie﻿—“you must come.”

===Chinese===
“是战胜了我自己，还是某一种背叛？”……那口盛着她晚装的长抽屉仿佛满是奇珍。她拽出一件自家缝的、淡蓝色修女绡（nun’s veiling）的缩褶小衬衫——先前觉得配纽兰兹嫌太寒素——罩在晨裙外头穿上了。它在她身上闪闪发亮。她飞快地洗了手，躲开穿衣镜的魅惑，心里盘点着自己的头发——从早上起就没碰过——盘点着这件外行手工的衬衫，它那生嫩的净蓝硬抵着那条粗粝的黑裙。擦手时回到梳妆台前，她发现那奇迹又更新了一遍。镜中迎面对住她的那个身影，裹在一层奇异的、调和万物的光晕里。她朝它望了片刻，就像望一幅不认识的画，带着安详的、无利害的静观。锣声轻轻进到房间里来，没有带来神经上忧惧的抽紧。她把那一记萦回的余音，织进一支木音乐盒里旧旋律的想象中的叮咚。赶在关掉煤气之前先开了门，她发现写字台上躺着一小束温室里养出来的铃兰（lilies of the valley）。……科里太太——“你一定得来。”

---

===Original===
Tucking them into her belt she went slowly downstairs, confused by a picture coming between her and her surroundings like a filmy lantern slide, of Portland Bill lying on a smooth sea in a clear afterglow.﻿ ﻿…
“Quite a madonna,” said Mrs. Staple-Craven querulously. She sat low in her chair, her round gold head on its short stalk standing firmly up from billowy frills of green silk﻿ ﻿… “a fat water-lily,” mused Miriam, and went wandering through the great steamy glasshouses at Kew, while the names that had been murmured during the introductions echoed irrelevantly in her brain.
“She *must* wear her host’s colours sometimes,” said Mr. Corrie quickly and gently.
Miriam glanced her surprise and smiled shyly in response to his shy smile. It was as if the faint radiance that she felt all round her had been outlined by a flashing blade. Mrs. Craven might go on resenting it; she could not touch it again. It steadied and concentrated; flowing from some inexhaustible inner centre, it did not get beyond the circle outlined by the flashing blade, but flowed back on her and out again and back until it seemed as if it must lift her to her feet. Her eyes caught the clear brow and smooth innocently sleeked dark hair of a man at the other end of the table﻿—under the fine level brows was a loudly talking, busily eating face﻿—all the noise of the world, and the brooding grieving unconscious brow above it. Everyone was talking. She glanced. The women showed no foreheads; but their faces were not noisy; they were like the brows of the men, except Mrs. Craven’s. Her silent face was mouthing and complaining aloud all the time.

===Chinese===
她把它们别进腰带，慢慢下楼去，一幅图景插到她同四围环境之间，像一张薄纱的幻灯片，搅得她恍恍惚惚——波特兰角（Portland Bill）卧在平滑的海面上，浸在一片澄澈的余晖里。……

“活脱脱一位圣母哩。”斯特普尔-克雷文太太愠愠地说。她低低陷在椅子里，那颗圆圆的金脑袋插在短短的梗子上，从绿绸那波浪般层叠的褶裥里稳稳地竖起来……“一朵胖睡莲。”米丽亚姆出神地想，人已游荡进邱园（Kew）那些雾气腾腾的大温室，介绍时低低报过的那些名字，全不相干地在她脑子里回响。

“她有时候也总该戴一戴主人家的徽色嘛。”科里先生轻快而温和地说。

米丽亚姆把她的惊讶瞥了过去，为回应他那羞怯的微笑，自己也羞怯地笑了。仿佛她感到环拥周身的那圈微光，被一道闪亮的锋刃勾出了边界。克雷文太太尽可以一味嫉恨下去；她再也碰不到它了。它安定下来，凝聚起来；从某个取之不竭的内在中心流出，却流不出锋刃勾出的那一圈，只在她身上流回来，又流出去，又流回来，直到仿佛非把她托起身来不可。她的眼睛捉住了桌子另一端一个男人清朗的额头，和他那光滑的、无辜地抿顺的黑发——那两道细而平直的眉毛底下，是一张高声谈笑、忙着吃的脸——全世界的喧嚣都在那里，而它上头，是那道冥想的、含悲的、无所自觉的额头。人人都在说话。她扫了一眼。女人们一概不露额头；可她们的脸并不喧哗；它们像男人们的眉额一样，唯独克雷文太太的除外。她那张沉默的脸，自始至终嚅动着，高声抱怨着。

---

===Original===
“Old Felix has secured himself the best partner,” Miriam heard someone mutter as she made her fluke, a resounding little cannon and pocket in one stroke. Wandering after her ball she fought against the suggesting voice. It had come from one of the men moving about in the gloom surrounding the radiance cast by the green-shaded lamps upon the long green table. Faces moving in the upper darkness were indistinguishable. The white patch of Mrs. Corrie’s face gleamed from the settee as she sat bent forward with her hands clasped in front of her knees. Beyond her, sitting back under the shadow of the mantelpiece and the marking board was Mrs. Craven, a faint mass of soft green and mealy white. All the other forms were standing or moving in the gloom; standing watchful and silent, the gleaming stems of their cues held in rest, shifting and moving and strolling with uncolliding ordered movements and little murmurs of commentary after the little drama﻿—the sudden snap of the stroke breaking the stillness, the faint thundering roll of the single ball, the click of the concussion, the gentle angular explosion of pieces into a new relation and the breaking of the varying triangle as a ball rolled to its hidden destination held by all the eyes in the room until its rumbling pilgrimage ended out of sight in a soft thud. It was pure joy to Miriam to wander round the table after her ball, sheltered in the gloom, through an endless “grand chain” of undifferentiated figures that passed and repassed without awkwardness or the need for forced exchange; held together and separated by the ceremony of the game. Comments came after each stroke, words and sentences sped and smoothed and polished by the gloom like the easy talking of friends in a deep twilight; but between each stroke were vast intervals of untroubled silent intercourse. The competition of the men, the sense of the desire to win, that rose and strained in the room could not spoil this communion. After a stroke, pondering the balls while the room and the radiance and the darkness moved and flowed and the dim figures settled to a fresh miracle of grouping, it was joy to lean along the board to her ball, keeping punctual appointment with her partner whose jaunty little figure would appear in supporting opposition under the bright light, drawing at his cigarette with a puckering half-smile, awaiting her suggestion and ready with counsel. Doing her best to measure angles and regulate the force of her blow she struck careless little lifting strokes that made her feel as if she danced, and managed three more cannons and a pocket before her little break came to an end.

===Chinese===
“老费利克斯给自己稳稳谋得了最佳搭档。”米丽亚姆听见有人咕哝，这时她正打出自己的侥幸球——一杆之内，一记脆响的双球连击（cannon）外加落袋（pocket）。跟在自己的球后头信步游走时，她同那个声音的暗示较着劲。声音来自幽暗里走动的男人们中间的一个——绿罩灯把光投在长长的绿呢台面上，四围便环着那圈幽暗。上方黑暗里浮动的一张张脸，分辨不出谁是谁。科里太太坐在长靠椅上，身子前倾，双手扣在膝盖前面，脸上那一小片白亮从暗里泛出来。再过去，深深缩在壁炉架和记分板的阴影里的，是克雷文太太，一团模糊的软绿和粉腻的白。其余的身影都在幽暗里站着或动着；站着的那些警醒而沉默，球杆发亮的杆身持定在歇息的姿势里；那些身影挪移着、走动着、闲步着，互不相撞、秩序井然，待小小的戏剧收了场，便低低地议论几句——击杆突然的一声脆响划破寂静，那只孤零零的球低低的雷一般的滚动，冲撞的一声咔嗒，那些小球儿温文地循着棱角迸散开来、结成一种新的关系，不断变动的三角阵随之破开，一只球滚向它隐秘的目的地，满屋的眼睛都押在它身上，直到它辘辘的朝圣之旅在视线之外一声轻钝的响里完结。对米丽亚姆来说，跟着自己的球绕台游荡是纯粹的快乐——藏身幽暗之中，穿行在一场没有尽头的“大连环”（grand chain）里，那些分不出彼此的身影来了又去、去了又来，没有半点局促，也不需要勉强的应酬；被游戏的仪式聚拢，又被它隔开。每一杆之后都有评点，词句让幽暗催得又轻又滑又亮，像挚友们在深浓的暮色里随意闲谈；而两杆之间，是一段段辽阔的、不受搅扰的静默的交契。男人们的竞逐，满屋升腾绷紧的那股求胜之心，败坏不了这场神交。一杆打罢，她端详着那些球——屋子、光亮和黑暗流转移动，朦胧的身影们重新落定，成就又一局分组的奇迹——然后俯身顺着台沿向自己的球贴过去，同她的搭档准时赴约：那是个轻快利落的小小身影，会出现在亮光底下策应的对位上，嘬着香烟，嘴角皱起半个微笑，等着听她的主意，也随时备着参谋。她竭尽全力地测量角度、拿捏力道，出手却是一记记漫不经心的小小挑杆，让她觉得自己仿佛在跳舞，又在小小的一轮连击收场之前，又成了三次双球连击、一次落袋。

---

===Original===
“It must be jolly to smoke in the in-between times,” said Miriam, standing about at a loss during a long break by one of her opponents.
